# Java quirks, bugs, and improvement candidates

This port is **behavioral**: it reproduces freerouting (Java, v2.3.0 baseline)
exactly, including its bugs, so that parity against the Java outputs is the
acceptance test. Every deliberate reproduction of a Java defect is recorded
here, with the Java location, where the Rust port pins it (a test or comment),
and what a fix would look like once parity is established.

Rules:
- **Parity is proven and is no longer the acceptance test (Plan 9).** A row's `status`
  says whether the port still reproduces it. Fixing a row means editing its status cell
  and adding a `// fixed: T<n> (#id)` comment beside — never deleting — the `// Java bug:`
  marker, **at every site**, with the row's own id in the parentheses. *(This bullet read "Do not fix these in the port until parity is proven on the
  fixture corpus" for Plans 1-8; Plan 9 Task 0 replaced it. The old rule made porting bugs
  distinguishable from intentional fixes, which is exactly what it was for, and it retired
  the day the last parity gate went green.)*
- Every task that finds a new one **appends a row** here (the task reviewer
  checks for it).
- **The `status` column, last on every table** (Plan 9 Task 0, recommendation 13):
  `pinned` = reproduced and covered by a test/comment, and the port still reproduces it;
  `fixed: T<n>` = the port no longer reproduces it, Plan 9 task `<n>` fixed it, and a
  `// fixed: T<n> (#id)` marker naming **this** row sits beside the `// Java bug:` one at
  every site — which `crates/fr-core/tests/register.rs` checks rather than trusts;
  `totalized` = Rust returns a value where Java crashes (documented divergence, not a bug
  fix); `candidate` = suggested improvement, not a Java bug; `keep` = deliberate behaviour
  on its own merits (survey §9.1) that Plan 9 is **forbidden** to "fix".
  A row whose status reads `pinned (keep in part — …)` is a row Plan 9 may fix in one
  respect and may not in another; the parenthesis says which half is which, and the
  leading word is the status a gate reads.
  A `fixed: T<n>` row whose fix went to a **milestone bench** for adjudication (ruling BP12)
  carries the verdict after a comma — `fixed: T2, accepted: M1 (BV)` — so the register says
  not only that the port stopped reproducing the defect but that the corpus agreed and which
  ruling closed it. The leading `fixed: T<n>` token is unchanged and is what the gate reads.
- **The register keeps every row, including every fixed one.** It is a description of the
  *Java* program, and that is what makes it useful upstream. Nothing is ever deleted here.

## Reproduced Java bugs and quirks (`pinned`)

| # | Java location | What Java does | Rust location | Suggested fix (post-parity) | status |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | `Direction.compareTo` / `IntDirection.compareTo` | Not antisymmetric when one operand is `NULL`: `RIGHT.compareTo(NULL) == -1` but `NULL.compareTo(RIGHT) == 0`. Also `(Big,Int)` and `(Int,Int)` dispatch arms disagree at the zero direction (asymmetric negation count). | `int_direction.rs` `compare_to` (no `Ord` impl on purpose); `direction.rs` cross-arm test | Define NULL as strictly less than every real direction; then `Ord` becomes lawful. | pinned |
| 2 | `IntDirection.turn45Degree(factor)` | `factor % 8` is negative for negative factors → falls to `default` → returns `(0,0)`. | `int_direction.rs` `turn_45_degree`, test | Use floor-mod (`rem_euclid`). Check callers never pass negatives first. | pinned |
| 3 | `Direction.toString` | Tests `compareTo(RIGHT) == 0`, which is true for `NULL`, so `NULL` prints `"RIGHT"`; the `"NULL"` branch is dead. | `int_direction.rs` `Display` | Use `equals`; print `"NULL"`. Diagnostic only. | pinned |
| 4 | `Point.getInstance(BigInteger x,y,z)` / `Vector.getInstance` | Reduces by `z` after checking only `x mod z == 0`; `y` is integer-divided even when not divisible (truncation). | `point.rs` / `vector.rs` `from_big`, tests | Check both `x` and `y` before dividing. Audit callers for reliance. | pinned |

Reachable via `TileShape.nearestBorderPoint(Point)` (`ShapeTraceEntries`, `ShapeEntrySide`). | `rational_point.rs`, pinning test | Change to `subtract`, matching `IntPoint`. | pinned |

| 10 | `FloatPoint.roundToGrid` | Uses `Math.rint` (ties-to-even) while every other rounding site uses `Math.round` (half-up). | `float_point.rs` (documented) | Probably harmless; unify if grid snapping ever misbehaves at `.5`. | keep — §9.1: an accepted divergence with no observable consequence |

| 12 | `Simplex.removeRedundantLines` via `Direction.equals` | Two distinct zero directions are *not* equal in Java (reference shortcut only), so a doubly-degenerate input keeps 4 lines / `dimension()==0`; the Rust structural `NULL == NULL` gives 3 lines / `dimension()==1`. | 21 | `LineSegment.stairApproximation` / `45` | `new IntPoint[2*stairCount+1]` throws `NegativeArraySizeException` for `width <= 0` or non-finite; zero callers in the Java tree. | `line_segment.rs` `.max(0)` (deferred minor: prefer assert) | Reject non-positive width up front. | pinned |
| 22 | `Polyline(Line[])` → `removeOverlaps` (Polyline.java:147-155) | Once the loop has decremented `newLength` to 0, `tmpArr[newLength - 1]` reads index -1 → `ArrayIndexOutOfBoundsException`. Reached by ~11% of random line arrays drawn from a small pool of equal/opposite lines (280k-case sweep); the six lines `h, v, h, v, h, v` over the same two axes are a minimal case. **Not totalized:** `PolylineTrace.combine_at_end` (PolylineTrace.java:303-311) tests `joinedPolyline.lines.length != newLineCount` right after the constructor, so a zero-line polyline and a thrown exception are *different* observable outcomes — Java's pass-level `catch (Exception)` aborts the pass. | `polyline.rs` `remove_overlaps` / `from_lines` return `Err(PolylineError::NormalizationIndexUnderflow)`, so Plan 2's `combine_at_end` can abort the pass like Java's pass-level catch; pinning test **Fixed in the port at Plan 9 Task 6, in its own commit and with its own measurement.** `remove_overlaps` (`crates/fr-geometry/src/polyline.rs`) takes the `newLength >= 1` guard the trailing access at Polyline.java:160 already has, in the same `guard && test` shape, with `// fixed: T6 (#22)` at the loop. Note the *effect* is the opposite of the trailing access's — a failed guard there skips the append, here it performs one — and that is right: with nothing kept there is no "last kept line" for the overlap test to ask about, so the answer is "no overlap" and the line is kept; decrementing is what the taken branch does to undo an overlap that was found, and none was. The `Result` return **stays** even though `NormalizationIndexUnderflow` is now unconstructible: it is the module's public normalisation signature, threaded through `from_lines`, `from_lines_in_place`, `shorten` and `BoardError::Normalization` into three crates, and collapsing it would be an interface change with no behavioural content. That the variant is unreachable **is** the fix — Java's pass-level `catch (Exception)` has nothing left to catch. **The 280 000-case sweep, re-run on both sides** (4..8-line arrays from a pool of four equal/opposite axis lines, seeded `JavaRandom(1)`): before, 20 630 threw / 177 678 normalised to 0 lines / 81 692 to 3 lines; after, **0 threw** / 198 308 / 81 692. `177 678 + 20 630 = 198 308` and the 3-line column is identical, so the change is **strictly additive**: every input that used to throw now normalises to the empty polyline and nothing that already succeeded moved. (The row's ~11 % is its own sweep's rate; this pool and length distribution give 7.37 %.) **Golden movement: none.** `cargo nextest run --workspace` moves exactly two tests, both direct pins of the throw and neither a committed reference — `fr-geometry`'s `from_lines_skips_parallel_and_overlapping_lines` and `fr-board`'s `from_lines_err_propagates_as_board_error_normalization`, whose jar row (`S14 combine=threw ArrayIndexOutOfBoundsException`) is kept verbatim while the port's answer is asserted positively: the combine now completes (`Ok(true)`), the joined trace is absorbed (items `[3,2,1]` -> `[2,1]`) and the degenerate head cancels, leaving `six` as `[(4000,2000), (4000,5000)]` where it used to be the untouched five-corner polyline. Every reference family — `reference_parity`, `batch_parity`, all three `cli_e2e` stems and the two-run byte-identity check — is untouched, and `tests/reference/` is byte-clean. Directed test `remove_overlaps_on_a_degenerate_array_normalises_to_a_literal` (`crates/fr-geometry/tests/polyline.rs`) carries the hand-computed answer for the row's own minimal case, the six lines `h, v, h, v, h, v`: the empty polyline. | Guard the loop with `newLength >= 1`, as the trailing access at line 160 already is. | fixed: T6 |
| 23 | `Polyline(Point, Point)` (Polyline.java:69) | Recomputes the *end* closing direction as `fromCorner → toCorner`, a verbatim repeat of line 66, where `Polyline(Polygon)` (line 50) uses `last → second-last`. The two constructors therefore hand back opposite (geometrically identical) closing lines for the same pair of points. | `polyline.rs` `from_two_points`, test `the_closing_lines_are_perpendicular_to_the_end_segments` | `Direction.getInstance(toCorner, fromCorner)`; check nothing depends on the current orientation first. | pinned |
| 24 | `TileShape.rotateApprox` (TileShape.java:692-695) | The two-corner branch builds `new LineSegment(currentPolyline, 0)`, but that constructor's valid range starts at 1, so it stores three `null` lines and `toSimplex()` throws a `NullPointerException`. ~2% of random degenerate 2..4-line shapes reach it. | `tile_shape.rs` `rotate_approx` (returns `Simplex::EMPTY`) **Fixed in the port at Plan 9 Task 6 — with a correction to the survey's premise.** The guard was **already here**: `rotate_approx`'s two-corner arm (`crates/fr-geometry/src/tile_shape.rs`) has answered `Simplex::EMPTY` since Plan 2 rather than building the three-`null` `LineSegment`, so there was no `#[should_panic]` to invert and Task 6 changes no code at this site. What it adds is the `// fixed: T6 (#24)` marker, the register status, and a *binding* directed test. The port deliberately does **not** take the suggested "pass 1 instead of 0": a two-corner polyline bounds no area, so a real segment built from it would be a shape with no inside, and matching the zero-corner arm immediately below is the more defensible reading. Test `the_two_corner_branch_answers_an_empty_simplex` (`crates/fr-geometry/tests/polyline.rs`) pins a witness found by the same kind of random sweep this row's ~2 % came from — four lines over the pool -4..=4 — and proves the branch is the one taken by rebuilding `rotateApprox`'s own corner polygon and asserting it has exactly two corners, rather than trusting `EMPTY` to imply the path (the zero-corner arm returns `EMPTY` too). | Pass 1 instead of 0. | fixed: T6 |
| 25 | `Polyline.cornerCount()` (Polyline.java:178-181) | Returns -1 for an empty polyline, which then flows into `new IntPoint[cornerCount()]` in `rotateApprox` (`NegativeArraySizeException`) and into `boundingBox(0, -2)`. | `polyline.rs` `corner_count` saturates at 0 **Fixed in the port at Plan 9 Task 6 — with a correction to the survey's premise.** As for #24, the guard was **already here** and Task 6 changes no code: `corner_count` (`crates/fr-geometry/src/polyline.rs`) returns `usize` and saturates, so the port has never been able to answer Java's -1, and neither consumer the row names — `new IntPoint[cornerCount()]` and `boundingBox(0, -2)` — is expressible. Added: the `// fixed: T6 (#25)` marker, the register status, and `an_empty_polyline_has_no_corners_and_no_negative_array` (`crates/fr-geometry/tests/polyline.rs`), which reaches the empty polyline the way Java does (two parallel lines normalising below three) and pins 0 corners and an empty corner list. | Return 0, or make the empty polyline unrepresentable. | fixed: T6 |

| 33 | `IntPoint.equals` (IntPoint.java:40), `RationalPoint.equals` (RationalPoint.java:80), `IntVector.equals` (IntVector.java:34), `RationalVector.equals` (RationalVector.java:63) | All four open with a `getClass() != other.getClass()` guard, so a value in one representation is **never** `equals` to the same value in the other: `new IntPoint(1,1)` is not equal to the rational point `(2,2,2)`. Live behaviour depends on it — `Direction.getInstance(Point, Point)` returns `null` off this relation, and `Point.translateBy` short-circuits on `vector.equals(Vector.ZERO)` where `Vector.ZERO` is an `IntVector`, so a rational zero vector does *not* take the shortcut. | `point.rs` `impl PartialEq for Point` and `vector.rs` `impl PartialEq for Vector` are hand-written (not derived from the inner values) to reproduce the class gate; `Hash for Point` hashes the discriminant to stay consistent. The two tighteners compare a *translated* line against its original to detect a sub-unit translation that did not move the line, which is exactly where the geometric and structural tests disagree; using `==` there would silently disable the guard and loop. All three tightener sites are pinned by `crates/fr-router/tests/tightener.rs`'s `lineeq` rows (`onLine`, `subUnit`, `halfInt`), each of which was verified to fail when the site is mutated to `==`. The caller list is duplicated on `equals_geometric`'s doc comment. | Override `hashCode` consistently with the geometric `equals`, or rename the geometric test and leave `equals` structural, as this port does. | pinned |

| 36 | `ClearanceMatrix.removeClass(int)` (ClearanceMatrix.java:325-352) | Rebuilds every surviving row with `new Row(currentOldRow.name)`, whose constructor allocates a **fresh zeroed** `maxValue` array (ClearanceMatrix.java:338,388) — the old per-row maxima are dropped — while the matrix-wide `maxValueOnLayer` is not recomputed at all and keeps maxima that may have belonged to the *removed* class. So after a removal `maxValue(classI, layer)` answers 0 for every class until the next `setValue`, and `maxValue(layer)` can answer too high. Verified against the Java class body under JDK 23: removing class 1 from a 3-class matrix whose class 2 had a row max of 50 leaves `maxValue(1, 0) == 0` and `maxValue(0) == 50`. Reachable from `BoardRules.removeClearanceClass` (BoardRules.java:347), whose only caller is `gui/windows/routing/WindowClearanceMatrix.java:274` — so the bug is GUI-only in Java today, and the port reproduces it for a `BoardRules::remove_clearance_class` that no `board`/`autoroute`/`drc`/`io` code reaches either. | `crates/fr-board/src/rules/clearance_matrix.rs` `ClearanceMatrix::remove_class` (`// Java bug:` comment); tests `remove_class_drops_the_row_and_column_and_loses_every_row_maximum` and `remove_class_zero_shifts_the_default_class_down` | Recompute both `row.maxValue` and `maxValueOnLayer` from the surviving entries after the removal (they are running maxima, so they can only be restored by a full rescan). | pinned |

Reachable from `ShapeTree.statistics` (ShapeTree.java:111-136), which calls it for every leaf of `toArray()`; a one-item tree therefore cannot be `statistics()`-ed. | 39 | `MinAreaTree.removeLeaf(Leaf)` (MinAreaTree.java:120-179) | Removing the **same leaf twice** silently destroys the tree. The first call nulls the leaf's `parent` (MinAreaTree.java:125); the second call therefore takes the `parent == null` branch (MinAreaTree.java:130-134), decrements `leafCount` a second time and sets `root = null` — discarding every other leaf, with no exception and no log line. The `null` guard at MinAreaTree.java:121-123 only catches a `null` reference, not an already-removed one. | **Deliberate divergence.** `crates/fr-board/src/datastructures/shape_tree.rs` `ShapeTree::remove_leaf` asserts the `LeafId` still names a live leaf and panics otherwise (test `removing_a_leaf_twice_panics`). The arena cannot reproduce Java's version without reproducing the silent data loss with it, and a search tree that quietly forgets every item would surface far later as a mis-routed board. With #106's visited set added it **returns in 80 steps**, answering `normalize = true` and leaving **no trace at all** — the ladder is 2-connected, so after `split` cuts each rail at the two interior rungs every edge lies on one of the three square faces, and `removeIfCycle(t)` removes `getConnectionItems(t)` — the whole chain through `t` — rather than `t` alone, so the cascade consumes it instead of thinning it to a spanning tree. `crates/fr-board/tests/trace_normalize.rs` `a_two_rail_four_rung_ladder_normalizes_and_terminates` is that literal answer and replaces the `#[ignore]`d `a_four_rung_ladder_never_finishes_normalizing`; the suite now has no ignored test. `a_four_rung_ladder_stops_when_the_stop_check_trips` and `normalize_all_traces_checked_stops_on_the_ladder` are re-pointed rather than deleted — the ruling-4 `StopCheck` seam is still live and still answers `Stopped` when the budget is under the walk. The terminating ladders did not move: rungs 1/2/3 cost 12/22/22 steps and answer `[4]`, `[5]`, `[6, 5]` with the fixes and without them (the `28` this row used to quote was a stale Plan 3 figure). This is safe because `ShapeTree.toArray()` has exactly one caller in the Java source (`ShapeTree.statistics`, an unported diagnostic), so no query this crate ports ever observes raw tree shape; every real query canonicalizes its result into a `TreeSet`/`BTreeSet` ordered by `(object id, shape index)`, independent of the tree's internal layout. | 80 | `PlanarDelaunayTriangulation.splitAtInnerPoint` allocates three edges it never uses (PlanarDelaunayTriangulation.java:740-743) | `Edge[] newEdges = new Edge[3]; for (int i = 0; i < 3; i++) newEdges[i] = new Edge(this.getCorner(i), corner);` — and `newEdges` is never read again. The dead ones are not free: every `Edge` constructor draws `newEdgeId()` (:267-270), and edge id **is** the sort key of the `TreeSet<Edge>` `getEdgeLines` returns (:110, :419-421). Dropping the loop therefore renumbers every subsequent edge. Visible directly in the edge ids of a triangulation: `1,2,3` for the bounding triangle, then `7,8,9` for the first inner split — `4,5,6` are the dead ones. It does **not**, as it first appears, reorder the result: the three dead ids are always allocated contiguously and immediately before the same split's live edges, so deleting the loop shifts every later id by a constant and leaves the relative order of the live edges unchanged. Confirmed by mutation — with the loop deleted all eight `p2t13` differential modes stay byte-identical to the JVM. | 82 | Delaunay's bounding triangle is finite, and its in-circle predicate silently fails on axis-aligned input (PlanarDelaunayTriangulation.java:65-69, :476-484) | The algorithm starts from a bounding triangle at `Limits.CRIT_INT` (2^25) with corners `(C,0)`, `(0,C)`, `(-C,-C)` — two of them exactly on an axis — and decides every edge flip with `FloatPoint.insideCircle`, whose `circleCenter` is a slope formula dividing by `p2.x - p1.x`, by `p3.x - p2.x` and by `slope1`. For `p1.circleCenter(p2, p3)` exactly **three of the six** possible coordinate coincidences make it degenerate, JVM-verified: `p1.x == p2.x` → `(NaN, NaN)`, `p1.y == p2.y` → `(finite, NaN)`, `p2.x == p3.x` → `(NaN, NaN)`. The other three — `p1.x == p3.x`, `p1.y == p3.y`, `p2.y == p3.y` — give a finite centre and behave normally. When it does degenerate, `insideCircle` answers `false` = "legal, do not flip". Because two bounding corners sit on the axes, ordinary axis-aligned input triggers it. **Reachability, all JVM-verified.** (a) *Edges are lost.* The four corners of a square come back as **4** edges, not the 5 a triangulated quadrilateral has — for `{(0,0),(1000,0),(1000,1000),(0,1000)}` the missing edge is the top side, and the failing test is `P4.insideCircle(B1, P3, P2)` with `P2=(1000,0)`, `P3=(1000,1000)` sharing `x`, so `slope2 = -1000/0 = -Inf` and `circleCenter(B1,P3,P2) = (NaN, NaN)`. Nudging the quad off the axes (`{(0,0),(1000,7),(1013,1000),(11,1007)}`) restores all 5. Square grids, i.e. pad rows/columns and BGA fields, lose edges at every size: 2x2 → 4/5, 3x3 → **15/16**, 4x4 → 31/33, 5x5 → **50/56**, 6x6 → **75/85**, 7x7 → 116/120. (b) *Usually harmless, sometimes not.* A grid stays **connected**, so `NetIncompletes`' MST still reaches every pad — it just may pick a longer airline than the true nearest neighbour. But the graph can also come apart: on a 7x7 grid of 1000-unit pitch centred on the origin, drawing 20 of the 49 points at random, **27 of 200,000 sets** (~1.4e-4) produce a *disconnected* result, and the rate climbs with density (40 of 49 points: 228/50,000 ≈ 0.5%; 100-unit pitch, 20 points: 339/50,000 ≈ 0.7%). Witness, confirmed on the JVM: for the 20 points `(-3000,-3000) (-3000,-2000) (-3000,2000) (-2000,-2000) (-2000,1000) (-2000,2000) (-1000,0) (-1000,1000) (0,-3000) (0,2000) (0,3000) (1000,-2000) (1000,1000) (1000,2000) (1000,3000) (2000,0) (2000,1000) (2000,3000) (3000,0) (3000,1000)` Java returns 40 edges and the pad at **`(-1000,1000)` gets no incident edge at all** — every one of its would-be neighbours shares an x or a y with it, so `NetIncompletes` never emits an airline for that pad and the net's incompleteness is under-reported. (Sets with all-non-negative coordinates did not disconnect in ~1.5M trials; straddling the origin, which is where a real board's outline sits, is what makes it reachable.) | `crates/fr-board/src/datastructures/delaunay.rs` reproduces it exactly (it shares `fr-geometry`'s `FloatPoint::inside_circle`, whose `None` centre already maps to `false` for the same reason). Tests `square_pins_javas_four_edges` and its control `skewed_quadrilateral_has_the_five_edges_a_square_should_have`; `p2t13` modes 1 and 5 match the JVM byte for byte. | Use an exact `inCircle` determinant over `Point` (the same `BigInt` machinery `sideOf` already uses) instead of the float circumcentre, and push the bounding triangle out to a coordinate no input can be collinear with. **Plan 5 must NOT do this while porting `NetIncompletes`.** Both changes alter which airlines the ratsnest emits and therefore what the autorouter routes, so they are a post-parity change: fix it only once DRC parity against the Java engine is proven, and re-baseline the ratsnest expectations in the same commit. | pinned |
| 83 | `ClearanceMatrix.setValue`/`getValue` index J-then-I, not I-then-J (ClearanceMatrix.java:93-102, 163) | `setValue(classI, classJ, layer, value)` writes `row[classJ].column[classI].layer[layer]` — the **second** argument selects the row and the **first** selects the column, the reverse of the obvious reading of the parameter names. `setValue` writes exactly one cell, so the matrix is only symmetric if a caller writes both orders. Every Specctra writer/reader in the Java tree does (`io/specctra/parser/Structure.java:768-769,785-788`, `io/specctra/parser/Network.java:644-673,749-781`, each `setValue(a, b, …)` immediately followed by `setValue(b, a, …)`), so DSN-imported boards never observe the asymmetry — but `io/kicad/KiCadJsonReader.java:153,161` writes only one order (`setValue(1, clNo, …)` and `setValue(idxA, idxB, …)`), so a **KiCad-sourced board's clearance matrix is genuinely asymmetric**: `getValue(idxA, idxB, layer)` and `getValue(idxB, idxA, layer)` can legitimately differ. Not a bug — Java is internally consistent, `getValue` reads back through the identical `row[classJ].column[classI]` path — but it is the single easiest transcription error when porting the accessor, since nothing in the method signature hints at the reversal. | `crates/fr-board/src/rules/clearance_matrix.rs` `ClearanceMatrix::index` takes `(class_i, class_j, layer)` and computes `(class_j * class_count + class_i) * layer_count + layer`, matching Java's row/column order exactly; the struct's module doc states the J-then-I rule and the KiCad-asymmetry consequence up front. No dedicated test pins the asymmetry itself (Plan 3's DSN/KiCad readers are what can exercise it end-to-end), but `set_value`/`get_value`'s existing round-trip tests would catch a swapped index immediately. | None — document the convention prominently (done) so Plan 3's KiCad reader does not "fix" the apparent asymmetry by writing both orders, which would silently change a KiCad-sourced board's clearances from what Java computes. | keep — §9.1: Java is internally consistent and a KiCad-sourced matrix is genuinely asymmetric; writing both orders would change the clearances of every KiCad board for no defect |

Net effect: a DSN file truncated in the middle of an unrecognised scope is read as a **successful** parse of whatever came before the truncation — `DsnReader.readBoard` reports `BoardReadResult.Success` with a partial board, not a parse error. | `crates/fr-dsn/src/parser/scope_parameter.rs` `skip_scope` returns `Ok(false)` on end of file (fix round 1 — an earlier revision wrongly turned it into `Err(DsnError::UnexpectedEof)`, which would have made a truncated file fail instead of succeed); `read_scope_generic`'s own end-of-file check (a few lines below `skip_scope`) is what returns `Ok(true)` — and now records the truncation on `ReadScopeParameter::truncation`, which `DsnReader::read_board` turns into `BoardReadResult::Partial`. Tests: `crates/fr-dsn/tests/scopes.rs` `a_truncation_inside_an_unknown_scope_reports_partial` (which replaces `read_scope_generic_returns_ok_true_when_truncated_inside_an_unknown_scope`) and `a_truncated_dsn_reads_as_partial_not_success`. | None needed for parity; a "fix" here would mean `DsnReader::read_board` should report a truncated file as an error rather than a partial success, which is a product decision, not a bug fix, since callers may already rely on getting back whatever routing data survived. **Done (T4), and the roadmap settled the product decision the other way:** not a refusal but a **third state**, `BoardReadResult::Partial`, carrying the surviving board *and* the diagnostic. `skip_scope` still answers `Ok(false)` and the generic loop still answers `Ok(true)`; what changed is that the read no longer *claims* to be complete. Every consumer updated in the same commit; `fr_core::load::parse_board_result` accepts it and appends the diagnostic to the warnings it already surfaces. No golden moved — the `corpus-read-results.txt` variant golden still reads `Success` for all 105 corpus files. | fixed: T4 |

| 93 | `Circle.boundingBox` treats `coor[0]` as a radius, everything else treats it as a diameter (Circle.java:56-64) | `bounds = {coor[1] - coor[0], coor[2] - coor[0], coor[1] + coor[0], coor[2] + coor[0]}` — the centre plus and minus the **whole** of `coor[0]`. (Circle.java:15-18's own javadoc says "coor[0] is the radius of the circle", which is what the bounding box follows and the rest of the code contradicts.) The box therefore comes out **2x too wide and 2x too tall**. Reachable, not dead: `Structure.createBoard` unions the outline shapes' `boundingBox()` and derives both the board bounding box and the DSN scale factor from the union (Structure.java:1159-1167), so a design whose outline is given as a `(circle …)` is sized — and scaled — from a doubled box. | `crates/fr-dsn/src/parser/geometry.rs` `DsnCircle::bounding_box` reproduces it verbatim with a `// Java bug: Circle.boundingBox …` marker; the `DsnCircle` type doc records which of the two readings each member follows. | 94 | `Structure.createBoard`'s overflow loop truncates `scaleFactor` to zero (Structure.java:1189-1203) | `int scaleFactor = Math.max(scopeParameter.resolution, 1);` … `while (5 * maxCoor >= Limits.CRIT_INT) { scaleFactor /= 10; maxCoor /= 10; }`. `scaleFactor` is an **`int`**, so `/= 10` truncates: the loop runs `ceil(log10(5 * maxCoor / CRIT_INT))` times, and that exceeds the resolution's decimal digits exactly when `5 * maxAbsCoor >= Limits.CRIT_INT`, i.e. for any boundary coordinate at or above **6,710,886 DSN units — whatever the resolution**. `scaleFactor` then hits `0`, `new CoordinateTransform(0, 0, 0)` is built without complaint, and quirk #89's IEEE division makes every coordinate the DSN/SES writers emit `Infinity`/`NaN` while every coordinate the *reader* transforms collapses to `0` (so the board's whole outline degenerates and `bounds` becomes the bare `(-1000,-1000)..(1000,1000)` offset box). JVM-verified against `tools/freerouting-2.3.0.jar`: `(resolution um 10)` with `(boundary (rect pcb 0 0 10000000 10000000))` reads as **`Success`** with `coordinate_transform.board_to_dsn(1.0) == Infinity` and `boundingBox = -1000,-1000 .. 1000,1000`. The control, `(resolution um 10000000)` with `(rect pcb 0 0 10000 10000)`, stops at `scaleFactor == 100` (`board_to_dsn(1.0) == 0.01`) — the same loop, one order of magnitude short. Both change the board coordinates of every over-large design, so it is a post-parity change; re-baseline the affected fixtures in the same commit. **Done (T4):** `double`, not clamped — clamping would defeat the loop's own purpose (keeping `5 * maxCoor` under `CRIT_INT`) for exactly the boards that need it. `f64` and `int` division agree wherever `resolution / 10^n` is integral, so **no fixture was re-baselined: nothing moved**. **Exactly one corpus board enters the loop**: `Issue676-ch32v-tx118s.dsn`, at `resolution 1 000 000` with `maxCoor = 21 590 000`, so `5 * maxCoor = 1.08e8 >= 2^25` and the loop runs one iteration — `int` `1000000 / 10 == 100000`, `f64` `100000.0`, the same scale factor, and the fixture MATCHes on all five sweep modes. `tests/reference/cli-large-outline` (`Issue035-ReadPlaceScope.dsn`) was checked **first** per the task brief and reaches only `maxCoor = 2 940 050`, i.e. it does *not* enter the loop and is not a fixture for this row; the directed test therefore carries a synthetic 6.71 M-unit outline with a hand-computed expectation instead. | fixed: T4 |

The step is a function of that triple and of `result` (the last only under `StopConnectionOption::FanoutVia`, whose `isFanoutVia(result)` reads the partially built set), so a no-progress repeat is a genuine closed loop, while a repeat that did make progress is allowed to continue — `ignoreItems` only ever *skips* a contact, so a larger `result` can send the walk somewhere it has not been. | 141 | `GsonProvider.GSON`'s `Strictness.LENIENT` governs only the *textual* pass, and non-finite floats are refused in both directions (GsonProvider.java:12-20 with RouterSettingsTypeAdapterFactory.java:50, :56) | `setStrictness(Strictness.LENIENT)` (`:19`) reaches only the first of the reader's two passes. `RouterSettingsTypeAdapterFactory.read` builds a `JsonElement` tree with the lenient textual reader (`:50`), then re-reads that tree through `delegate.fromJsonTree(tree)` (`:56`) — a **fresh `JsonTreeReader` at default strictness**. So the textual dialect is permissive while the value conversion is not. JVM-verified (`crates/fr-settings/tests/data/JProbe.java`). Block D, the permissive half: an unquoted name (`{max_passes: 42}`), a single-quoted name, a leading `//` comment, and a quoted scalar coerced to the field's type (`{"max_passes": "42"}` -> `42`, `{"strict_drc": "true"}` -> `true`) are all accepted; a trailing comma is not. Block I adds a duplicate key (last wins, `1,2` -> `2`), a fractional literal truncated into an `Integer` field (`1.9` -> `1`), and an empty, whitespace-only or literal-`null` document (all three answer a **null** `RouterSettings`, `:51-53`) — while a *quoted* `"null"` is still a `JsonSyntaxException`. **This list is illustrative, not exhaustive**; the rule is "Gson's reader is more permissive than strict JSON", and nothing pins the boundary. Block H is the strict half: `NaN`, `Infinity`, `-Infinity` and the quoted `"NaN"` all throw `JsonIOException: MalformedJsonException: JSON forbids NaN and infinities` on **read**, and the same builder never calls `serializeSpecialFloatingPointValues()`, so `toJson` throws `IllegalArgumentException` on **write** (block B4). | **The non-finite half is reproduced; the lenient-reader half is not.** `crates/fr-settings/src/json.rs` `RouterSettings::to_json_string_pretty` returns `SettingsError::Json` for a non-finite float, exactly where Gson throws, and `serde_json` already refuses to read one — so the two **agree** in both directions (`non_finite_floats_are_refused_like_gson`, `non_finite_floats_are_refused_on_the_read_side_too`). `from_json_str` is `serde_json`, i.e. strict JSON, and carries a `// not ported: Strictness.LENIENT` marker with the reason. **That reason changed in Plan 8 Task 5** and the row is corrected in place: it read ~~"`JsonFileSettings` — Gson's only reader of this type in headless Java — is out of scope (spec section 2)"~~, and scan ruling R7 ruled `JsonFileSettings` back **in** (`crates/fr-settings/src/sources/json_file.rs`, priority 10, wired to `--settings <file>` and to a working-directory `freerouting.json`). So the port now *does* have a live reader of a `freerouting.json`, and it is strict where Gson is lenient. The divergence stays acceptance-only for one reason that survives the change: `JsonFileSettings.loadSettings` swallows **every** exception into an empty `RouterSettings` (`JsonFileSettings.java:55-62`), so where Gson coerces `{"max_passes": "42"}` into `42` and the port reports an error, the port's *outcome* is an empty settings object and Java's is `max_passes = 42` — a **missing** contribution at priority 10, never a different one. `sources::json_file::tests::a_router_object_the_reader_rejects_is_swallowed` pins it. Plan 8's MCP `settings` input likewise arrives as strict JSON. That divergence is acceptance-only (error vs value), never a different value; the one shape the port cannot express at all is Gson's *null* `RouterSettings` for an empty document, because `from_json_str` returns `RouterSettings`, not `Option`. | 147 | `NetIncompletes.Edge.compareTo` is not injective, so the candidate-airline `TreeSet` silently drops edges (NetIncompletes.java:360-379 with `:174`, `:183`) | The comparator subtracts `lengthSquare`, then — only when that is 0 — `fromCorner.x`, `fromCorner.y`, `toCorner.x` and `toCorner.y`, and returns `Signum.asInt` of the result. Its own comment (`:364-366`) says the four coordinate tie-breakers exist "so that edges with the same length as another edge are not skipped in the set". They do not achieve that: two candidate airlines between **different** items whose corners coincide pairwise agree on all five keys, compare 0, and `TreeSet.add` returns false for the second — which is exactly the case of a via stacked on a pad, or two pads at one location. The dropped edge is one fewer candidate for Kruskal, so the spanning tree may join the two groups through a longer edge or, if the drop took the only edge between them, not join them at all and understate `incompleteCount`. On finite coordinates — everything a real board produces, since a `FloatPoint` here always comes from an `IntPoint` — the comparator is a consistent total preorder, so Java's red-black `TreeSet` and the port's B-tree drop the *same* edge, the later one offered, and the reproduction is exact. `Signum.asInt` is a `> 0 / < 0 / else` ladder (Signum.java:35-42) and so also maps **NaN** to 0, which is a second, weaker failure: an edge whose coordinates make a subtraction NaN compares equal to every other edge, and what that costs depends on **insertion order** — offered first it swallows every later edge and the set ends with one element, offered later it is simply the edge that is dropped. The relation is not transitive there (two finite edges still order against each other), so a red-black tree and a B-tree may legitimately reach different elements to compare against and drop *different* edges. The port therefore reproduces Java's **comparator**, and claims parity with Java's **tree** only in the consistent case. | `crates/fr-drc/src/net_incompletes.rs` `impl Ord for Edge` (the `Java bug:` marker), transcribed including the `f64` subtraction and `Signum::as_int_f64` rather than `partial_cmp`; unit tests `an_exact_five_way_tie_drops_the_second_edge`, `a_nan_edge_swallows_or_is_swallowed_depending_on_insertion_order` and `edges_are_ordered_by_length_then_by_the_four_coordinates` in the same file. `Edge`'s `PartialEq` is defined through `cmp` rather than derived, so `Ord`'s `cmp == Equal` / `==` contract holds even where the comparator is wrong; `Eq` is asserted for `BTreeSet`'s sake with the reflexivity caveat documented on the impl, exactly the position Java's `Comparable` contract is in | Break the remaining tie on the two `NetItem`s' identities — Java has no `Item.compareTo`-free handle, but the array index is already to hand at the construction site, and `Integer.compare(fromIndex, otherFromIndex)` after the four coordinates makes the comparator injective without changing any ordering that is currently meaningful. | 156 | `ObstacleExpansionRoom.getId` packs the shape index into the item id with an **or**, so an item with 1024 or more tree shapes aliases (autoroute/expansion/ObstacleExpansionRoom.java:48-51) | The body is `(this.item.getId() << 10) \| this.indexInItem`. `\|` never carries, so any `indexInItem >= 1024` spills straight into the item's bits: `id(item 1, index 1024)` is 1024, which is exactly `id(item 1, index 0)`, and `id(item 1, index 2048)` is 3072, which is `id(item 3, index 0)`. Test `obstacle_room_id_aliases_above_1023_shapes` becomes `the_java_obstacle_room_id_aliases_above_1023_shapes` and keeps every witness, and the new `every_expandable_id_is_stable_and_injective` (`crates/fr-router/tests/expansion_rooms.rs`) is the plan's binding property test: over a domain that deliberately includes `indexInItem >= 1024` and `itemId >= 2^21`, seven obstacle rooms plus the page grid plus two incomplete rooms plus a complete room all answer **distinct** ids, where Java's formula gives those same seven rooms **five**. **One correction to the fixture bank.** `docs/plan-9-prep/fixtures/task-8/expected-outcomes.md`'s second witness — `id(5, 1024) = 5120 \| 1024 = 6144`, aliasing `id(6, 0)` — is arithmetically wrong: 5120 is 4096 + 1024, so bit 10 is already set and the `|` changes nothing (`id(5, 1024) == 5120 == id(5, 0)`). | 160 | `SortedRoomNeighbours.SortedRoomNeighbour.compareTo` is **not a total order**, and the `TreeSet` it feeds silently drops elements it decides are equal (autoroute/expansion/SortedRoomNeighbours.java:719-762, `:54`, `:408`) | The comparator's keys are `touchingSideNoOfRoom`, then the sign of a difference of first-corner distances, then — only when the two first corners are *equal* — the sign of a difference of last-corner distances, then — only when **both** neighbours have `neighbourRoomTouchIsCorner` — a `Direction.compareFrom` of the two border lines, and finally `searchTreeObject.getId()` difference. Mixing those refinements breaks transitivity: a pair that reaches the direction branch can be ordered against a third element by id in a way the direction answer contradicts, and a pair that compares `Equal` can sit on opposite sides of a fourth element. Because the elements land in a `TreeSet` (`:54`, inserted from `addSortedNeighbour` at `:408`), a comparison that answers 0 makes `TreeMap.put` return the existing value and the new neighbour is **discarded** — so a door the room really has is never built. Which element is discarded depends on the comparison path the container's own shape produces, and therefore on the insertion order that `calculateNeighbours`' `List.sort` at `:204-213` fixes. JVM-verified: `scripts/differential/run.sh p6t3 3 42 0 2000` produces 481 such drops in 2 000 cases, `corner c=520` keeps five elements whose in-order walk is `id 1, 5, 2, 4, 5`, and `corner c=521` drops `add[3]`. | Reproduced: `crates/fr-router/src/autoroute/expansion/sorted_neighbours.rs` `SortedRoomNeighbour::compare_to` (transcribed line for line, `Signum::as_int_f64` included) over `crates/fr-router/src/java_tree_set.rs` `JavaTreeSet` — **a `BTreeSet` is not equivalent** and was measured diverging in both directions (a kept element vs a dropped one, and a different survivor order), which is why `java.util.TreeMap`'s red-black insertion is transcribed rather than reused from `std`. Tests `the_comparator_is_not_transitive_and_drops_the_same_neighbour_java_does`, `a_tie_on_geometry_falls_back_to_the_object_id` (`crates/fr-router/tests/sorted_neighbours.rs`) and the `java_tree_set.rs` unit tests; differential `p6t3` modes 1-3. **Fixed in the port at Plan 9 Task 8.** `SortedRoomNeighbour::compare_to` is now a **lexicographic** comparison of Java's own keys in Java's own order, with the three defects removed and `// fixed: T8 (#160, #161)` on the method. (1) `:729-733`'s inner `firstCorner().equals(other.firstCorner())` gate is gone — refining by the last corner only when the two first *corners* are the same point is strictly stronger than the distances tying, and two different corners equidistant from the compare corner (`(300,400)` and `(400,300)` at 500 from `(0,0)`) skipped the refinement, kept `deltaDistance == 0.0`, and fell to an id tie-break that answered `0` for two neighbours of the same object. (2) `c_dist_tolerance` no longer gates which key decides: a `<= 1.0` band that *selects* a key and then takes the exact sign of it is the textbook non-transitivity, so the keys are compared exactly and "tie" means equal. The constant has no reader left and is gone. **This is not a refinement of Java's order and an earlier draft of this row wrongly said it was.** Java has two bands: the **outer** one, on the first-corner key, is dead as a selector — its body is gated on exact point equality of the two first corners, which already forces the delta to 0, so a pair 0.5 apart enters the band, fails the inner test and is decided by the exact sign anyway. The **inner** one, `|delta| <= 1.0 && both ntc` on the *last*-corner key, is live, and removing it **reverses** pairs: two neighbours with equal first-corner distances, last corners 300.0 and 300.5 from the compare corner and both `neighbourRoomTouchIsCorner` are decided by `Direction.compareFrom` in Java — which may answer `Greater` — and by the exact last-corner distance here, which answers `Less`. The reversal is accepted deliberately: a tolerance that selects which key answers is precisely what makes the relation non-transitive, so a comparator that keeps it cannot be a total order at all. (3) The two corner flags are compared **before** the `Direction.compareFrom` refinement they gate, because a conditional key is enough to destroy transitivity on its own — measured on the generator below, case 118: `0 <= 1 <= 3` while `0 > 3` — and confining the refinement to one equivalence class makes it a genuine total order (`IntDirection::compare_from` is the circular order rotated to start at a reference that is then the same for every member of the class). Past Java's last key the remaining value fields are compared — `touchingSideNoOfNeighbourRoom`, both corners, and the neighbour's own shape — so `Equal` means "equal as a value" and a set can no longer drop a door the room really has; the shape key is what separates two tree shapes of **one item**, which carry the same object id by construction. `the_comparator_is_not_transitive_and_drops_the_same_neighbour_java_does` becomes `five_neighbours_at_one_corner_are_all_kept_and_sort` and `a_tie_on_geometry_falls_back_to_the_object_id` becomes `a_tie_on_geometry_no_longer_drops_the_neighbour`; both keep the jar's transcript verbatim beside the port's answer, which is a **known divergence from the jar authorized by this row** (the jar drops `corner c=521`'s `add[3]`; the port keeps it, and the two are separated at `touchingSideNoOfNeighbourRoom` — 3 against 1, two different sides of two different neighbour boxes). The new `the_neighbour_comparator_is_a_total_order` reproduces `p6t3` mode 3's generator in Rust down to the xorshift stream (`P6T3.java:132-153`, `:1084-1136`, seed 42, 2 000 cases, 8 000 neighbours) and asserts three things: **0 drops** in a `JavaTreeSet` **and** in a `BTreeSet`, antisymmetry over every pair, and transitivity over every ordered triple. The reproduction is provenance-checked against the jar's own stdout — case 520 draws `Box[1730,-384..3188,78]`, which is what `corner c=520 room=` printed. Measured by stashing the comparator and running the same test: the pre-fix tree drops **481** of 8 000 in a `JavaTreeSet` and **482** in a `BTreeSet` (the one-element difference is the container hazard itself), and fails transitivity at case 12; the post-fix numbers are 0 and 0. The regeneration is permanent only for a **complete** neighbour. So the skip's visible effect was nil and its invisible effect was the crash. What does move is the arena, and that is what the tests read: `Arena` never reuses an index, so `slot_count()` is the honest record of "this door was visited". Test `remove_complete_expansion_room_skips_incomplete_neighbours` becomes `a_door_onto_an_incomplete_room_is_not_skipped` and asserts that removing the four-incomplete-neighbour room constructs **2** rooms and **2** doors where it constructed 0 — the two neighbours whose `touchingSides` is not empty — while the live counts stay at Java's 7; it fails before the fix with `left: 0, right: 2`. `touching_sides_is_length_checked` is its sibling and asserts that the fixture really carries the short-array case (2 of the 4 doors) and that the call does not panic | Take an `ExpansionRoom` parameter (or cast, as `completeNeighbourRooms` does) **and** length-check `touchingSides` before indexing it. Neither path removes the room from anything, because it was never added to `completeExpansionRooms` — but `calculateNewIncompleteRooms` has already built doors from it to real incomplete rooms, so it stays **reachable** through them. The consequences are all silent: `clear` (`:308-312`), `validate` (`:642`), `getRoomsWithTargetItems` (`:623`) and `initConnection`'s net-dependent invalidation (`:102`) all walk the list, so an abandoned room is never validated, never invalidated when the net changes, and never removed from the search tree — and `completeExpansionRoom`'s `:426-432` scan can pick one as its `ignoreObject`, i.e. hand `completeShape` a room that is not in the tree it is querying. JVM-verified on the HEAD jar (`scripts/differential/java/probes/P6T6Probe.java` modes 1 and 5): mode 1 constructs nine rooms and lists six, and mode 5 shows room 1's two door neighbours are both abandoned rooms whose shapes are 1-dimensional. | Reproduced by keeping the two containers apart: `crates/fr-router/src/autoroute/maze/engine.rs` `AutorouteEngine::complete_expansion_rooms` is Java's list, and `ExpansionRoomStore::complete_rooms` is Java's heap — every method Java writes as a walk over `completeExpansionRooms` walks the list, and the field docs on both say why. The release is conditional on the id being the newest, so it can never hand the same number to two rooms that both survive. On `one_obstacle_run` the six rooms' ids read `[6, 12, 23, 29, 36, 38]` where the jar reads `[1, 2, 6, 7, 8, 9]`. **An earlier draft of this row said `[1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6]`, which was true of this fix in isolation and stopped being true three commits later**: #156 + #167 + #158 put obstacle rooms, incomplete rooms and drill pages on the *same* counter, which is what makes the ids injective across the four kinds, so they are no longer dense. Density was never what this row buys. What it buys is that a room which is never committed consumes no id, and what the search tree's ordering needs is that the ids strictly increase in creation order — which `the_complete_room_list_is_exactly_the_rooms_in_the_tree` asserts directly. **(b) The abandoned room leaves no doors behind — but by `detach_all_doors`, not `removeAllDoors`, and that distinction is measured.** `removeAllDoors` deletes the incomplete room on the far side of every door it walks (`:610-612`, recursing through `removeIncompleteExpansionRoom`), and the rooms hanging off the room `addCompleteRoom` abandons are the engine's **expansion frontier** — Java leaks them into `incompleteExpansionRooms` and the maze search expands through them. With `remove_all_doors` on that path six of `crates/fr-router/tests/locator.rs`'s cases stop finding a connection at all, which is a connectivity regression and the opposite of what this row is for. `ExpansionRoomStore::detach_all_doors` unlinks the doors from both sides and deletes nothing, which removes the abandoned room's *reachability* — all the row asks for: `completeExpansionRoom`'s `:426-432` scan can no longer pick it as `ignoreObject` and hand `completeShape` a room that is not in the tree it is querying. What #169's crash was doing was shielding it on this path. | 193 | Three HEAD-only "stale index during routing" guards that silently `continue`, with no upstream counterpart (autoroute/maze/MazeSearchEngine.java:653-668; autoroute/ItemAutorouteInfo.java:57-79; autoroute/maze/MazeTraceShover.java:62-67) | Each one admits, in its own comment, that an item's tree-shape indices can go stale **while the search is running** — "prevents warning when indices become stale during routing", "trace was modified during routing", "Stale indices can occur when traces are modified during routing (pull-tight, shoving, etc.)". `MazeSearchEngine` skips a target door whose `treeEntryNo` is out of range and then skips it again if `getTraceConnectionShape` answers `null`; `ItemAutorouteInfo.getExpansionRoom` **resizes** `expansionRoomArr` when the shape count has changed, copying `min(old, new)` entries across, and returns `null` for an index still out of range; `MazeTraceShover` refuses a `traceCornerNo` beyond `lines.length - 2`. None of the three exists in freerouting 2.3.0 — they were added at HEAD, which is this plan's parity jar. Together they mean the router treats its own search tree as something that changes underneath it, which is a design admission, not a bug. **Plan 9 Task 17 measured them, and the "load-bearing: the corpus reaches all three" claim is half right — it reaches all three *sites* and trips none of them.** Instrumented (`crates/fr-router/src/autoroute/instrument.rs`, read-only, gated by `P9T17_STALE`) over all eight batch stems: **0 fires in 1 101 064 guard evaluations** (re-measured at Plan 9 Task 8, whose nine fixes moved the **visit** counts on four of the eight stems: **0 fires in 1 160 973** — the denominator moved, the finding did not; `crates/fr-router/tests/stale_index.rs`'s `MEASURED` is the live table and its `// T8:` comments bisect each row to the fix that moved it) — G1a `treeEntryNo` out of range 34 516 visits, G1b `getTraceConnectionShape` null 34 516, G2 resize 489 077, G2 out-of-range 489 077, G3 `traceCornerNo` 53 878, every fire count zero on every stem. **Why:** the staleness is real and reachable (a directed case shortens a trace through `PolylineTraceSearchTreeAdapter.replaceGeometry` and the recorded index *is* then out of range) but cannot reach a guard, because `replaceGeometry:38`'s `clearDerivedData()` nulls `autorouteInfo` in the same call that changes the shape count — so `expansionRoomArr` and the shape count can never disagree — and `Item.getTreeShape:212-226` re-derives once before answering `null`; and because every router board write lands **outside** the search it could invalidate (`reduceTraceShapesAtTiePins` at `init:970-971`, before the first room exists — and it reduces **nothing** on any corpus board; the ripup and `removeTraceTails` at `autorouteConnection:260-263`, after the maze search returns; the inserter and `optChangedArea` later still), while doors and rooms live one connection inside one `AutorouteEngine`. Full measurement, mutation census and caveats: `docs/plan-9-prep/stale-index-report.md`. | Ported verbatim, because the parity jar has them: `crates/fr-router/src/autoroute/maze/expand.rs` (`MazeSearchEngine::expand_to_room_doors`, the two `continue`s, with `treeEntryNo`'s `< 0` arm noted as unreachable for a `usize`), `crates/fr-router/src/autoroute/item_info.rs` (`get_expansion_room`, including the resize-and-copy), and `crates/fr-router/src/autoroute/maze/trace_shover.rs` (hazard N, the module docs). Task 17 added `// T17:` visit and fire recorders at all five exits and mutation tags at the five router board writes; the guards themselves are untouched, and `crates/fr-router/tests/stale_index.rs`'s `instrumentation_changes_no_board_byte` pins a byte-identical SES on all eight stems with the counters on. The measurement is pinned exactly (fires **and** visits, per stem) by `…::the_three_guards_are_counted_on_every_router_stem`, and the staleness itself by `…::a_shortened_trace_makes_a_recorded_index_stale_and_clears_the_array_that_held_it`. | **Was:** "find out why the indices go stale and fix *that*". **Measured (Task 17): on this codebase, at HEAD, over this corpus, they do not** — the ordering of `init`, `autorouteConnection` and `clearDerivedData` has already closed the window. **Recommended: leave the guards and document them.** They are five branches on paths already walked 1.1 million times, they are the parity jar's own code, and zero fires is a corpus result rather than a proof — a board with a tie-pin reduction, or any future reuse of an `AutorouteEngine` across connections, reopens the window. The two rejected alternatives are a generation-checked `(index, generation)` handle (changes the data model away from the jar's for a condition measured at zero, and cannot be validated against it) and re-deriving the index at use (changes routing wherever a guard fires, which is nowhere, so it buys nothing and risks a divergence the corpus cannot detect). **Open first: answered (controller, non-isolated git, post-review of this row).** The three guards arrived in mechanical refactor commits — `363a8a54` (responsibility extractions), `8de1cebc` and `cf0dc2ed` (renames), `9a4fa374` (checkstyle) — none of them a bug-fix or a crash report; this corroborates the row's own inference from the two `FRLogger.warn` sites above ("somebody's log was noisy", not "somebody's board crashed"). Status stays `pinned`. | pinned |

| 201 | `getHash`'s javadoc says it hashes the **trace** state; it hashes the whole item graph (board/facade/BasicBoard.java:163, board/facade/BoardSnapshotManager.java:57 against :26-43) | `BasicBoard.getHash`'s comment reads "Returns an MD5 hash of the board trace state" and `BoardSnapshotManager.getHash`'s "Returns an MD5 hash of the board trace-state profile", and `serialize(true)`'s own reads "the historical basic trace profile". All three are wrong about the same line: `serialize(true)` makes **three** `writeObject` calls (:31-33), and the third writes `board.itemList` — every item, of every kind, with every non-`transient` field. So a board whose traces and vias are identical but whose pins, keepouts or outline differ hashes differently, which is the opposite of what all three comments promise. Harmless in the jar (the three readers only ever ask whether two hashes are equal), but not harmless downstream: this comment is exactly what justified this port's original trace-and-via-only `structural_hash` (Plan 2), under which **every trace-free board hashed alike** and three `BoardHistory` decisions diverged from the jar. | `// Java bug:` on the hash section of `crates/fr-board/src/board/snapshot.rs`'s module doc, above the ruling-AH audit table that enumerates what the digest really reaches; `crates/fr-board/tests/snapshot.rs` `trace_free_boards_with_different_items_no_longer_collide` is the test that would have caught the wrong reading, and `crates/fr-router/tests/board_history.rs` `trace_free_boards_are_distinguishable` is the inverted Task 2 pin | Reword the three comments to say "of the board's item list", or make `serialize(true)` write only the traces its name claims — the second changes every hash the router computes. | pinned |
| 202 | `--max-items` silently disables the optimizer stage; `--max-passes` does not (autoroute/pipeline/AutoroutePassRunner.java:212-221 against AutorouteBatchLoop.java:267-271, read by RoutingPipeline.java:116-118) | `StoppableThread`'s stop flag is a **three-state** enum, not a boolean: `requestStop()` writes `ALL` (StoppableThread.java:23-25) and `requestStopAutoRouter()` writes `AUTO_ROUTER_ONLY` (:33-37). The two queries differ by one comparison — `isStopRequested()` is `== ALL` (:28-30), `isStopAutoRouterRequested()` is `!= NONE` (:40-42) — and the pipeline reads both. The pass runner reaches `settings.maxItems` (:212-221) and calls `requestStop()` (**ALL**); the batch loop reaches `settings.maxPasses` and calls `requestStopAutoRouter()` (**AUTO_ROUTER_ONLY**); and `RoutingPipeline.runOptimizationStage` returns early on `isStopRequested()`. So a run that stops on `--max-items` skips the optimizer stage entirely and writes an unoptimised board to the SES, while a run that stops on `--max-passes` optimises normally. Nothing in either message says so: the pass runner logs "Max items limit reached. Stopping auto-router." — the optimizer is not the auto-router. Measured on the HEAD jar by `scripts/differential/java/probes/P7T4Probe.java`'s `[sequences]` block (`seq=maxItems … optimizerStageWouldRun=false`, `seq=maxPasses … optimizerStageWouldRun=true`). | `crates/fr-router/src/pipeline/stop.rs` — the module doc's site table and `RouterStop::is_stop_requested`'s doc; the `// Java bug:` marker sits at the port's `maxItems` site — **landed in Plan 7 Task 9**, in `crates/fr-router/src/pipeline/pass_runner.rs`'s transcription of `:212-221`, and the `obligation:` line Task 4 left in `stop.rs` is discharged with it. `crates/fr-router/tests/pass_runner.rs` `max_items_requests_stop_all` is the second pin, on a real pass rather than on the probe transcript. `crates/fr-router/tests/stop_and_progress.rs` `max_items_stops_all_and_max_passes_stops_the_router_only` asserts both halves against the probe transcript, including that the optimizer stage would not run after a `maxItems` stop | Have `AutoroutePassRunner:219` call `requestStopAutoRouter()`, so both limits mean "stop routing" and neither silently cancels optimization; or say so in the log line. | pinned (keep in part — §9.1: the three-state stop `NONE`/`AUTO_ROUTER_ONLY`/`ALL` must not collapse to a bool) |
| 203 | `AutorouteBatchLoop`'s timeout handler is dead code (autoroute/pipeline/AutorouteBatchLoop.java:251-253) | The pass loop opens with `if (job != null && job.state == RoutingJobState.TIMED_OUT) { router.thread.requestStopAutoRouter(); }`. It can never do anything. `job.state` is only ever set to `TIMED_OUT` by the per-job monitor thread at `management/jobs/RoutingJobSchedulerActionThread.java:84`, and that thread has already called `job.thread.requestStop()` at `:75` — **thirty seconds earlier**, since `:76-83` waits out `GRACE_PERIOD` in between. By the time `TIMED_OUT` is readable the flag is therefore `ALL`, and `requestStopAutoRouter()` only upgrades `NONE -> AUTO_ROUTER_ONLY` (StoppableThread.java:33-37), so it is a no-op. The loop's own `while (… && !thread.isStopAutoRouterRequested())` condition at `:250` had already ended the loop for the same reason. Confirmed on the HEAD jar by `P7T4Probe`'s `seq=monitorTimeout … secondWasNoOp=true`. | `crates/fr-router/src/pipeline/stop.rs` — `RouterStop::request_stop_auto_router` and `RouterStop::poll_deadline` both carry the reasoning, and it is *why* `poll_deadline` performs `request_stop()` (ALL) rather than `request_stop_auto_router()`: the port has no monitor thread (controller ruling AI), so the poll must reproduce the monitor's **observable** action. `RouterStop::is_timed_out` exists only so `AutorouteBatchLoop:578-584` can report `TaskState::TimedOut`. Pinned by `crates/fr-router/tests/stop_and_progress.rs` `request_stop_auto_router_does_not_downgrade_all` and `the_deadline_requests_stop_all_like_the_monitor_thread` | Delete `:251-253`, or move the `TIMED_OUT` write in `RoutingJobSchedulerActionThread` ahead of the `requestStop()` so the handler has something to handle. | pinned |

| 211 | `RoutingBoard.reduceNetsOfRouteItems`' **trace** arm can strip *every* net from a route item in a single visit, because its `if (somethingChanged) break;` sits outside the net loop — the **via** arm's sits inside it (board/facade/RoutingBoard.java:1341 against :1310) | Both arms walk `for (int currentNetNumber : currentItem.netNumbers)` and call `removeFromNet(currentNetNumber)` for any net the contacts do not support. The via arm breaks that loop at `:1310-1312` as soon as `somethingChanged`, so a via loses **one** net per visit and the `while (somethingChanged)` restart re-tests `:1296`'s `netNumbers.length <= 1` guard before touching it again. The trace arm's break is at `:1341-1343`, *after* the net loop closes, so the loop runs to completion over the array it captured at `:1318` — which `removeFromNet` has meanwhile replaced (`Item.java:902-913` assigns a fresh array; the enhanced-`for` keeps the old one) — and calls `removeFromNet` once per unsupported net. A two-net trace whose contacts support neither net therefore ends the visit with **zero** nets, a state `:1296` would have refused to enter. Whether this is deliberate is not knowable from the source: the two arms are otherwise the same shape, and the trace arm's extra `for (int i = 0; i < 2; i++)` end-swap loop at `:1317` needs *a* break there, so the placement may simply have been inherited. **Measured**, `P2T11.java` mode 6: trace 4 on nets `{1, 2}` with start contacts via 2 (net 1) and trace 5 (net 2) — each carrying one of the two nets and lacking the other — answers `nets(4)=[]`. | **Port defect, found by the Plan 7 Task 8b review (S4) and fixed under the same ruling.** `Board::reduce_nets_of_route_items` (`crates/fr-board/src/board/query.rs`) had collected the trace arm's removal into an `Option` and `break`ed the net loop on the first one, so the port left **one** net where Java leaves none — and the `while something_changed` restart then skipped the item under `:1296`. It now collects a `Vec` and breaks only where Java breaks; the via arm is unchanged, because Java's via arm really does stop at one. Collecting and applying after the loops is exact: every test in the loops is about the *contact* (`containsNet`, `instanceof Pin`), never about the item's own nets, and `contacts` is recomputed only at `:1344`, which is unreachable once anything was removed. | 212 | `ItemRouteResult`'s improvement percentage divides two `int`s (autoroute/ItemRouteResult.java:59-65, the ratio at `:63`) | The constructor computes `improvementPercentage = (float)(viaCountBefore != 0 && traceLengthBefore != 0 ? 1.0 - (((viaCountAfter / viaCountBefore) + (traceLengthAfter / traceLengthBefore)) / 2) : 0)`. `viaCountAfter` and `viaCountBefore` are both `int` fields (`:8-9`), so the **via** term is an integer division that truncates towards zero — `0` whenever the re-route left fewer vias than it started with, `1` when the counts are equal — while the **trace** term beside it is a full `double`. Unit pins: `crates/fr-router/tests/optimizer.rs`'s `an_auto_router_only_stop_does_not_disable_this_stage` (the stage is not skipped) and `an_auto_router_only_stop_leaves_every_item_rejected` (six items visited, board hash unchanged, and the same board with a clean flag *does* move). | Reset the flag between stages — `runOptimizationStage` is the natural place, since it has already decided the optimizer may run — or make `autoroutePassesForOptimizingItem` read a stage-scoped flag rather than the job's. | 228 | `runBatchLoop`'s `scoreImprovement` uses `-1` as a sentinel that a real pass improvement can equal (autoroute/pipeline/BatchOptimizer.java:212-230) | `:215` assigns `scoreImprovement = -1` to mean "do not test the threshold this time — spend the increased ripup costs and take another pass", and `:220` tests `scoreImprovement != -1`. | 229 | **A port defect, not a Java one.** `BatchOptimizer.optRouteItem`'s failed-attempt restore is `routingBoard.undo(null)` (autoroute/pipeline/BatchOptimizer.java:509 → board/facade/BasicBoard.java:1233-1240 → :1262 / :1276 → searchtree/SearchTreeManager.java:39, :47), which replays the attempt's item changes **through the live search trees**; the port assigns back a cloned board (`crates/fr-router/src/pipeline/optimizer.rs:695-710`), which puts the trees' internal topology back exactly. Same leaves, different `MinAreaTree` shape — and `ShapeSearchTree45Degree.completeShape` (board/searchtree/ShapeSearchTree45Degree.java:152-274) is **order-dependent**, so the shape leaks | `undo` restores `components` and `itemList` and leaves everything else on the *live* board; `applyUndoRedoSideEffects` (`:1255-1286`) then removes every cancelled item (`:1262`) and inserts every restored one (`:1276`) in every tree, so the tree that comes back has the same leaf set as the pre-attempt tree with the parents re-paired in `MinAreaTree.positionLocate` order (datastructures/MinAreaTree.java:87-114). `completeShape` walks that tree with an `ArrayStack`, prunes each node against a `boundingShape` that **shrinks as obstacles are consumed** (`:157` against `:263-264`'s `result = newResult; boundingShape = newBoundingShape;`), and restrains the room against every obstacle that survives the prune. A different traversal order therefore restrains against a different obstacle **set**, and the completed free-space room comes out a different shape. Measured on `Issue558-dev-board` (`p7t8 item 1 7`): item `n=1` is `improved=false`, Java's undo fires **54** `MAT` tree ops the port does not, the next `completeShape` sees the same `n=1299` nodes with a different `TREEFP` hash, and by item `n=6` the port restrains one free-space room against an obstacle Java prunes — one differently-shaped room, one extra door on the obstacle room of trace 8587, one extra `shoveTraceRoom`, and one extra `ShapeTraceEntries.nextSubstituteTracePiece` id (**188** ids burned vs the port's **187**). `Issue026-J2_reference` at router `maxPasses = 2` is the same statement: item `n=3` is `improved=false`, **84** Java-only `MAT` ops, divergence at item `n=4` (511 vs 475). The shifted ids later flip `ReadSortedRouteItems`' strict-`<` tie (quirk #63) and the two boards then genuinely differ | **Fixed in port (controller ruling BA, Plan 7 Task 14c).** The restore is now `Board::undo_from_snapshot` (`crates/fr-board/src/board/snapshot.rs`), the port of `BasicBoard.undo` + `applyUndoRedoSideEffects`: it takes `components` and the item map's difference from the clone, cancels every item the attempt inserted or modified in place on the **live** trees in Java's descending-id order (`:1262`), re-inserts the pre-attempt ones in Java's order — the modified ones descending, then the delete list in *deletion* order (`:1276`) — and leaves every other field of the live board alone. The order comes from `crate::board::snapshot::UndoJournal`, the one-level port of `UndoableObjects`' `deletedObjectsStack` + `UndoableObjectNode.level`/`.undoObject`, fed by `Board::journal_insert` / `journal_remove` / `save_for_undo` at each of Java's call sites; `BatchOptimizer::opt_route_item` brackets the attempt with `Board::begin_undo_journal` and `discard_undo_journal`. **Evidence:** with the level-8 instrument on (`P7T8B_IDS=1 P7T14B_MAT=1 P7T14B_FP=1`) the `MAT`/`TREEFP`/`ITEMSEP` streams are now byte-identical over the whole run on both boards — 134 910 lines on `Issue558-dev-board` (`item 1 3`) and 89 629 on `Issue026-J2_reference` (`item 2 6`), where Task 14b measured 54 and 84 Java-only ops; `p7t8 <dev-board> item 1 20` and `p7t8 <j2> item 2 30` are MATCH. The instrument stays committed and off by default: `P7T14B_MAT` (`ShapeTree::insert_leaf`/`remove_leaf`, `crates/fr-board/src/datastructures/shape_tree.rs`), `P7T14B_FP` (`TREEFP` in `complete_shape_45`, `TINS8` in `SearchTreeManager::get_autoroute_tree`), `P7T14B_CS` (`CSHAPE`/`CROOM8`/`CSO`) and `P7T14B_MAZE` (`ADD`/`POPQ`/`OCC`/`EXPROOM`/`SHOVEROOM`/`CSTL`), each with a Java twin in **level 8** of `scripts/differential/java/p6t17b-bisect.patch`; `p7t8`'s two halves print an `ITEMSEP` separator under `P7T8B_IDS`. The record of what the clone-assignment restore rolled back — including `normalizeSuppressedNetNos`, `shoveFailingObstacle`/`shoveFailingLayer` and the over-broad autoroute-scratch clear the Task 14b review inventoried — is the table in `crates/fr-router/src/pipeline/optimizer.rs`'s module doc | **Done** (Task 14c): the second of the two candidates below was taken — keep the clone for the item state, replay `applyUndoRedoSideEffects`' `remove`/`insert` on the live `SearchTreeManager` in its order. The first (port `generateSnapshot`/`popSnapshot`/`undo` whole, with `UndoableObjects`' full stack) stays unported and unnecessary: `BatchOptimizer.optRouteItem` is the only headless caller and it never nests, so one level is the whole requirement, and `redo` has no headless caller at all. Java's own side of it is still worth recording: **`undo` is not shape-preserving**, so an "undone" board is not the board it was, and Java's next `completeShape` sees a tree it has never seen before — the port now reproduces that faithfully rather than fixing it | pinned |
| 230 | `RoutingJob.getCurrentPass()` under-reports the routing stage's own pass count by one on a `maxPasses`-capped exit, disagreeing with the final `TaskStateChangedEvent`'s own `getPassNumber()` for the identical quantity (autoroute/pipeline/AutorouteBatchLoop.java:270-276, :521, :572-584; core/RoutingJob.java:250-256) | `:270-274`'s cap check (`maxPasses != null && maxPasses > 0 && currentPass > maxPasses`) runs *before* `:276`'s `job.setCurrentPass(currentPass)`, and both sit inside the same `while` body. On a capped exit the aborted final iteration breaks at `:274` without ever reaching `:276` again, so `job.getCurrentPass()` keeps whatever the last **completed** iteration wrote — one less than `currentPass`, which `:521` had already incremented past the cap at the end of that completed iteration. The loop's own final `TaskStateChangedEvent` (`:572-584`, fired after the `while` exits) carries that same incremented local as its `passNumber`, so the job's field and the event's payload disagree by exactly one for what both APIs document as "the pass the routing stage stopped at" — a caller reading one and a listener reading the other see different numbers at the exact moment a `maxPasses` run stops. Measured live: `p7t9 full` on `Issue143-rpi_splitter.dsn` at `maxPasses = 1`, `job.getCurrentPass() == 1` against the same run's final router event's `passNumber == 2` | `PipelineResult::passes_run` (`crates/fr-router/src/pipeline/run.rs`) is the loop-local quantity (matching the event's `passNumber`), not `job.getCurrentPass()` — `scripts/differential/java/P7T9.java`'s `full` mode driver reads the router's own last `TaskStateChangedEvent.getPassNumber()` rather than `job.getCurrentPass()` for exactly this reason, after the mismatch showed up as the sole diff on the first `p7t9 full` run | Have `AutorouteBatchLoop.run` call `job.setCurrentPass(currentPass)` once more right before firing the final event (or fire the event with `job.getCurrentPass()` instead of the local), so the job's own bookkeeping and the event payload agree at every exit, capped or not. | pinned |
| 231 | `applyCopperToEdgeClearanceOverride`'s `:501-507` guard makes the **default** `router.copper_to_edge_clearance_um` the one value that can be ignored — every other value, including a value the user typed to mean the same thing, mutates the board (management/HeadlessBoardManager.java:466-552, settings/sources/DefaultSettings.java:78) | The guard is `if (usesDefaultEdgeClearanceValue && !usesFallbackOutlineClass) return;`, where `usesDefaultEdgeClearanceValue` is `abs(configured - 500.0) < 1e-9` and `usesFallbackOutlineClass` is `outline.clearanceClassIndex() == defaultAreaClassNo`. Read as a feature it means "do not overwrite an explicit DSN outline-clearance class with a default nobody asked for"; read as behaviour it means the option is **discontinuous at its own default**. On a board whose outline carries an explicit class (`router-rpi-splitter`'s `boundary`), `--router.copper_to_edge_clearance_um=500` leaves the board alone while `=500.000001` — or `=0` — appends a `board_edge` class, writes the value into its whole row *and* column on every layer and re-points the outline at it. And on the other **15 of the 16** corpus boards, where the DSN reader gives the outline the fallback AREA class, the guard cannot fire at all, so the default **does** mutate: the plain `-de <dsn> -do <ses>` run every parity reference is generated from carries a 500 µm board-edge keep-out. **Measured** (`probes/P7T15bProbe.java`, all 16 boards × 9 variants): `router-dac2020-bm01`'s clearance-matrix sum goes 13 000 → 63 000 and its outline class 1 → 3; `batch-empty-board`'s 0 → 30 000; `batch-strict-drc-cnh`'s 1 772 800 → 2 152 800. It reaches the output bytes: `jar -de Issue026-J2_reference.dsn -do x.ses -mp 3` writes **15 254 B**, the same run with `--router.copper_to_edge_clearance_um=0` **14 644 B**, differing from char 741. The only reason it is invisible in practice is that the method logs at `FRLogger.debug` (`:546-552`). | `Board::apply_copper_to_edge_clearance_override` (`crates/fr-board/src/board/clearance_override.rs`), reproducing the guard including the `1e-9` comparison; the seam that calls it is `fr_router::pipeline::prepare_board`. Pinned by `crates/fr-router/tests/clearance_override.rs` (the 16-board transcript replay) and `crates/fr-board/tests/clearance_override.rs::the_default_value_is_the_only_one_the_guard_can_ignore`. | Key the "leave an explicit class alone" decision on whether the value was **explicitly supplied** (the settings ladder knows: `RouterSettings.copperToEdgeClearanceUm` is `null` until a source sets it) rather than on whether it happens to equal the default, and log the mutation at `info` rather than `debug`. | pinned |

| 241 | `getFileFormat(byte[])`'s leading-newline shift loop never refills `buffer[5]`, so a file whose first six bytes are all CR/LF makes it spin for ever — and its own comment names the wrong bytes (core/RoutingJob.java:180-187) | The loop is `while (buffer[0] == 0x0A \|\| buffer[0] == 0x0D) { buffer[0] = buffer[1]; … buffer[4] = buffer[5]; }`. `buffer[5]` is never reassigned, so after *n* shifts `buffer[i] == original[min(i + n, 5)]`: the guard can only see a new byte for `n <= 5`, and from `n == 5` on `buffer[0]` is `original[5]` for ever. The loop therefore terminates iff some `original[n]` with `n <= 5` is not CR/LF, and hangs otherwise — at 0 % CPU, with no output, inside a method whose caller (`BoardFileDetails.setData:113`) has no timeout. The comment at `:180` says "if the first few bytes are 0x0A or **0x13**"; the code tests `0x0A`/`0x0D`. Two further consequences of the same never-refilled slot, both measured: **three** leading newlines already destroy a DSN (`"\n\n\n(pcb "` shifts to `28 70 63 63 63 63`, and the fourth byte must be `0x62`), and the loop strips CR/LF only, so a leading **space** or tab defeats every magic below it. Measured on the HEAD jar with a five-second watchdog: rows 35-37 of `crates/fr-core/tests/data/p8t1-job-model.txt` hang, rows 22-23 (the same six newlines followed by `{`) do not, because the JSON pre-check at `:156-164` returns first. | `// totalized:` at `fr_core::FileFormat::sniff_bytes` (`crates/fr-core/src/job.rs`). The loop is bounded at **five** iterations — exact, not conservative, by the `min(i + n, 5)` argument above — and the converged buffer (six copies of a CR or LF) sniffs as `UNKNOWN`. `FileFormat::java_shift_loop_hangs` is a public predicate saying which inputs Java spins on, and `scripts/differential/run.sh p8t1probe` pins it in **both** directions: the Java half runs every `SNIFF` row on a watchdog and prints `XDIFF java=HANG(>5000ms) rust=UNKNOWN`, so a row where the two sides disagree about hanging is a diff. | Refill `buffer[5]` from the stream (or, better, `skip` the CR/LF run before reading the window). Fix the comment to say `0x0D`. | pinned |
| 242 | `changeFileExtension` NPEs on **every** bare filename, and its already-matching return is a different kind of value from its other two (core/RoutingJob.java:352-374) | `:356` is `filePath.getParent().toAbsolutePath().toString()` and `:357` is `filePath.getFileName().toString()`, both **before** any branch. `Path.of("out.ses").getParent()` is `null`, so `changeFileExtension("out.ses", "ses")` throws — even though the answer would have been the argument itself. `Path.of("")` and `Path.of("/")` throw too. Separately, the three returns disagree about what they produce: `:362` returns `filePath.toString()`, i.e. **the caller's own, possibly relative, string**, while `:369` and `:372` build an absolute path out of `getParent().toAbsolutePath()`. Two smaller ones ride along: the lower-cased extension is compared but the **original** one's length is cut (harmless only because the two are always equal), and the new extension is never folded, so `("/tmp/out.ses", "SES")` answers `"/tmp/out.SES"`. | `// totalized:` at `fr_core::RoutingJob::change_file_extension`.
