| 273 | The DRC session import happens **after** the `.rules` re-parse, and the order changes the report (Freerouting.java:277-294 then `:296-329`) | The three writes to the board are the DSN (`:271`), the `.rules` file and then the session, in that order, so a `.ses`'s wires and vias are **created** against whatever the rules file installed and are then checked against it. **The mechanism is `defaultItemClearanceClasses`.** `RulesReader.read`'s `Keyword.RULE` arm → `applyRules` → `Structure.setClearanceRule` (io/specctra/RulesReader.java:277-281; io/specctra/parser/Structure.java:676-808) calls `appendClearanceClass(boardRules, currentPair[i])` for either half of a clearance-class pair that is not already in the matrix (`:756`, `:765`), and `appendClearanceClass` (`:826-840`) **writes the default net class's `defaultItemClearanceClasses`** for the four names `via`, `pin`, `smd`, `area`. `SesReader.processViaScope` reads `defaultItemClearanceClasses.get(ItemClass.VIA)` at **via-creation** time (io/specctra/SesReader.java:395-400) and `processWireScope` the `TRACE` slot (`:316-321`), so a via made *before* that write takes the old class and one made *after* takes the new one. The `(class …)` arm reaches the same field by a second route, `RulesReader.applyNetClass` → `Network.insertNetClass` → `addClearanceRule`/`getClearanceClass` (io/specctra/parser/Network.java:753, :815-825). **Measured, on the jar and on the port.** `Issue593-BBD_Mars-64.dsn` + its `.ses`, with a one-rule file `(rules PCB Issue593-BBD_Mars-64 (rule (clearance 400.0 (type smd_via))))`: rules-first gives `structural_hash 3938620220908954804` and **15** clearance violations, session-first gives `945128192703554816` and **0** — and the HEAD jar's own `-de <dsn> <ses> -dr <that file> -drc r.json` run reports **15 `holeClearance` entries**, i.e. the jar takes the rules-first board, as its source says it does. **Why the corpus is silent, and the narrower true statement.** A `(rule …)` scope only reaches `appendClearanceClass` when it names a class pair: `(clearance N)` with **no** `(type …)` returns at `:684-707` after `setDefaultValue` and never touches an item class; `smd_to_turn_gap` is diverted to `setPinEdgeToTurnDist` and `continue`s (`:713-717`); an unquoted name with no `_` and a quoted name whose remainder does not start with `_` both `continue` (`:729-745`); and a pair of names that *are* new but are none of the four magic ones lands in `appendClearanceClass`'s `switch` `default ->` arm, appending a matrix class and writing no item slot. `Issue593-BBD_Mars-64.rules`'s own four rules are `(clearance 200.0)`, `(clearance 125.0 (type smd_to_turn_gap))`, `(clearance 50.0 (type smd))` and `(clearance 250.0 (type "kicad_default"))` — one of each skipped shape, and not one of them reaches the write. So: **class-blind and unpaired clearances are order-insensitive; a `via`/`pin`/`smd`/`area`-typed pair is not.** Plan 8 label **D**. *(Task 7 first recorded this row the other way round, claiming the plan's label-D sentence — "Swapping the two changes the violation list" — was falsified because ~~"everything a `(rules …)` scope writes (the clearance matrix, the net classes, the padstacks, the via rules, the snap angle) is consulted at *check* time, and the one creation-time field is one no `(rules …)` arm writes"~~. **That is false**, by `Structure.java:826-840` above; the three hand-written probes behind it had chosen `(autoroute_settings …)` only, a `(class …)` block with no inner `(rule …)`, and the non-magic pair `wide_wide` — one miss of each kind. The plan's sentence stands, and the strike is kept here so a reader who arrives from the Task 7 report's §6 can see what replaced it.)* | Reproduced in Java's order by `crates/freerouting/src/commands/drc.rs` (`load_rules_file` then `load_session_file`, each citing its Java line range); the write itself is `fr_dsn::parser::structure::append_clearance_class`, a line-for-line transcription of `Structure.java:826-840`. Pinned through the binary by `crates/freerouting/tests/cli_e2e.rs::the_session_is_imported_after_the_rules`, which measures the **difference** on the `smd_via` file (15 vs 0), keeps `(clearance 400.0)` as the labelled class-blind **control** (463 = 463), and then requires the CLI's own report to carry the rules-first count; and against the jar by `p8t3 e2e`'s `rules-and-session` row, the only run that fills both optional slots — it compares the report bytes, the exit code and the six-line log of a `-de <dsn> <ses> -dr <rules> -drc <report>` invocation. | Nothing to fix: the order is the correct one. | 276 | The DRC report's `date` is the JVM's **default zone**, and the port writes UTC (io/kicad/KiCadDrcReport.java:70) | `this.date = ZonedDateTime.now().format(DateTimeFormatter.ISO_OFFSET_DATE_TIME)`, in the constructor and `final`. `ZonedDateTime.now()` takes `ZoneId.systemDefault()`, so the committed references carry `…T05:28:26.406471-07:00` — the machine that generated them, not the board. **This row is a PORT divergence, recorded not hidden** (the shape of #251 and #275): the port renders the same instant in **UTC**, so the offset is always `Z`. There is no route from `std` to the host's UTC offset — every one is an external dependency (which the plan forbids) or a `libc` call (which `#![forbid(unsafe_code)]` forbids) — so the choice is between a wrong offset and an honest one, and `Z` is a valid `ISO_OFFSET_DATE_TIME` offset id that every consumer parses. What is **not** divergent is the rendering: `ISO_OFFSET_DATE_TIME` differs from the `ISO_INSTANT` of `Instant.toString()` (the manifest's `generated_at`, quirk-free, Plan 8 Task 4) in exactly two ways and both are ported — the fraction is *minimal*, trailing zeros trimmed (`appendFraction(NANO_OF_SECOND, 0, 9, true)`, so `100_000_000` ns is `.1` where `Instant.toString()` writes `.100`), and `:ss` is **omitted** when second and nanosecond are both zero (`10:15Z` is a legal value). | `crates/freerouting/src/commands/drc.rs::report_date`, whose doc comment carries the same two rules and the same reason; unit-tested by `the_date_is_iso_offset_date_time_not_iso_instant` over six fractions plus the no-fraction and non-zero-second edges. **Nothing compares the field**: `parity::normalize_drc_json` and `scripts/normalize-drc.py` both **drop** `date` (plan-5 ruling 3 — a wall clock cannot be a parity surface), and `fr-drc` takes it as an injected `String` for the same reason (plan-5 ruling 5), so neither `p8t3 e2e` nor `crates/fr-drc/tests/reference_parity.rs` can see the difference. It is registered here precisely because it is therefore invisible to every gate. | Take the zone from a caller. `fr_drc::DrcReportOptions::date` is already an injected string, so a host that knows its offset (an MCP server, a GUI) can pass a local-zone rendering today without touching either crate; only the CLI hard-codes UTC, and only because it has no way to ask. | pinned |
| 277 | The `ParseError.detail` a malformed KiCad JSON produces is the **JSON parser's own** message, and the two parsers are not the same one (io/kicad/KiCadJsonReader.java:78, :746-749) | `readBoard` wraps its whole body in `catch (Throwable e)` and answers `new BoardReadResult.ParseError("json_payload", "Exception occurred: " + e.getMessage())`. On a syntactically invalid payload `e` is Gson's, so `{` yields `Exception occurred: java.io.EOFException: End of input at line 1 column 2 path $.`; on one of the four unguarded `null` lists `e` is a `NullPointerException` and the message is the **JVM's helpful-NPE string**, e.g. `Cannot invoke "java.util.List.isEmpty()" because "boardJson.layers" is null` — text the JIT composes from the bytecode, which no port can reconstruct. `GsonProvider.GSON` is also built `.setStrictness(Strictness.LENIENT)` (util/gson/GsonProvider.java:20), so Gson accepts unquoted keys, single quotes, comments and the bare tokens `NaN`/`Infinity` that `serde_json` rejects outright. | **This row is a PORT divergence, recorded not hidden** (the shape of #251, #275 and #276). `crates/fr-dsn/src/kicad/reader.rs` returns the same `location` for the same inputs and reconstructs the *shape* of the NPE message from the same two facts (`npe(invoked, receiver)`), so the four unguarded lists match Java byte for byte; only the syntax-error branch differs, and `crates/fr-dsn/tests/kicad_reader.rs`'s `XDIFF` table carries the one row (`json-truncated`) with Java's exact text beside the port's. The LENIENT half is unreachable **through this port** by construction: an input `serde_json` rejects never reaches section 2, so no arm downstream can see a `NaN` resolution or an unquoted key. | Nothing to fix in Java. | 279 | `KiCadJsonReader.readBoard` catches **`Throwable`**, so a `StackOverflowError` or an `OutOfMemoryError` comes back as a parse error about the file (io/kicad/KiCadJsonReader.java:76, :746-750) | The `try` opens immediately after the null checks and closes around the whole 680-line body, and the handler is `catch (Throwable e)`, not `catch (Exception e)`. | 280 | The net **numbers** of every auto-registered net are `java.util.HashSet`'s iteration order, i.e. a function of `String.hashCode` (io/kicad/KiCadJsonReader.java:456, :490-496) | `readBoard` collects every net name referenced by a pad, a conduction area, a trace or a via into a `java.util.HashSet<String> referencedNets`, then iterates it with an enhanced-for and calls `boardRules.nets.add(netName, 1, false)` for each one the `nets` list did not declare. `Nets.add` numbers nets `1, 2, 3, …` in call order (Nets.java:89), so the hash order **is** the numbering, and the numbering is what every later `netNumbers[]`, DSN `(net …)` scope and SES wire carries. It is deterministic (`String.hashCode` is specified, and `-XX:hashCode` does not touch it) but it is not the file's order: `Issue649-kicad_ecc83-pp_input_board_v1.json` declares no nets at all and its thirteen pad nets come back `unconnected-(P7-Pad1), unconnected-(P6-Pad1), unconnected-(P8-Pad1), Net-(P2-P1), …`, nothing like the pads' declaration order. **The `HashSet` is not the only consumer of the hash order on this path, and the second one is not a numbering.** `readBoard:343`'s `netClassIndexMap` is a `HashMap<String, Integer>` and `resolveNetClassIndex:972-976` walks its `entrySet()` with an `equalsIgnoreCase` fallback — so on a board declaring two net classes that differ only in case, *which class a net is assigned* is the bucket layout's answer too. Computed from `HashMap`'s own model: `"Power"` and `"POWER"` at capacity 16 come back `POWER` first **whichever order the file declares them in**, so the jar's answer is independent of the document. The measured peak bucket over each fixture's `referencedNets` set — an earlier revision of this row said *"the two largest fixtures … both peak at a bucket of **2**"*, which is **not** the measured value — is `interf-u` **4** (173 nets, capacity 256), `complex-hierarchy` **3** (52, 128), its session export **3** (48, 64), both `ecc83-pp` **2** (13, 32) and `corney-island` **1** (5, 16). The conclusion is unchanged and the margin is two steps, not one: 4 against the 9 an actual treeify needs. The figures are re-derived by the same model `the_hash_iteration_order_matches_the_jvm` validates against the JVM, and the table is repeated at the `java_hash_iteration_order` site. The same helper serves `netClassIndexMap.entrySet()` at `:972-976`. **Fixed in the port at Plan 9 Task 7.** `readBoard`'s `referencedNets` is `ReferencedNets` (`crates/fr-dsn/src/kicad/reader.rs`), an insertion-ordered set, so an auto-registered net takes the next number at its **first reference** — the register's own suggested `LinkedHashSet`, and the second consumer, `:972`'s `netClassIndexMap.entrySet()`, moves to a declaration-ordered `Vec<(String, usize)>` with it. That leaves `java_hash_iteration_order`, `java_string_hash`, `JavaStringSet` and `JavaStringMap` with **no call site**: they carry a `// dead: killed by #280, deleted at T24` header and an `#[allow(dead_code)]`, and Task 24 deletes them (ruling BP16 — a shim is removed only after its fix has merged). **The `debug_assert!` guarding treeification is deleted**, with its deletion named at the site: it guarded a *numbering*, and nothing reads the function's answer any more, so the Plan 8 hand-off's §6-task-9 hazard is closed rather than deferred. **Measured, `Issue649-kicad_ecc83-pp_input_board_v1.json`'s thirteen pad nets, old -> new:** `unconnected-(P7-Pad1)` 1->7, `unconnected-(P6-Pad1)` 2->6, `unconnected-(P8-Pad1)` 3->13, `Net-(P2-P1)` 4->3, `Net-(U1B-K)` 5->9, `unconnected-(P5-Pad1)` 6->5, `Net-(U1A-K)` 7->4, `Net-(P4-PM)` 8->12, `Net-(P3-P1)` 9->1, `Net-(U1A-G)` 10->8, `Net-(P4-P1)` 11->11, `GND` 12->2, `Net-(P1-PM)` 13->10 — a permutation, asserted as one by `the_auto_registered_nets_take_first_reference_order_not_java_hash_set_order`. Directed tests `crates/fr-dsn/tests/kicad_nets.rs::{net_numbers_follow_declaration_order, an_auto_registered_net_takes_the_next_number_at_first_reference, a_declared_net_keeps_its_position_and_the_rest_follow}`, the first deriving its expectation from the JSON independently of the reader. `the_hash_iteration_order_matches_the_jvm` is deleted by name. **Golden movement: `tests/reference/cli-kicad-ecc83-json/route.ses`** — the routed wiring is byte-for-byte the same **five** `(net …)` scopes on the same paths, and the file is the same 3 812 bytes; two of the five moved position, which is what the 36-line diff is. | 281 | `readBoard` computes six values it never reads, one of which is a whole object graph (io/kicad/KiCadJsonReader.java:118-120, :446, and four DTO fields) | `:118-120` builds an `io.specctra.parser.LayerStructure` — its own comment says "dummy or map layer names if needed" — and `grep -n specctraLayerStructure KiCadJsonReader.java` finds only the declaration. `:446`'s `int maxNetNo = boardJson.nets.size();` is likewise never read; the dereference is not dead, though, because `boardJson.nets` is unguarded there exactly as `layers` is at `:105`. Four DTO fields are parsed and never consulted: `OutlineJson.clearance` ("outline/edge clearance class mapping", KiCadBoardJson.java:90 — `:310` hard-codes `outlineClearanceNo = 1` instead), `NetClassJson.netNames` (a net's class comes from `NetJson.className`), `NetJson.id` (numbers come from `Nets.add` — see #280) and `LayerJson.index` (the list position is used). Section 8 also computes `defViaDrill` / `viaDrill` (`:369-378`, `:414`) and shapes the via padstack from the **diameter** alone, so the drill never reaches the padstack. | `crates/fr-dsn/src/kicad/reader.rs` carries a `// not ported:` marker for `specctraLayerStructure` and for `maxNetNo` (with the note that its dereference is *not* dead), and `crates/fr-dsn/src/kicad/dto.rs` declares the four fields with a "never read by `readBoard`" line each — they are parsed because Task 10's writer has to write them back, which is what makes the DTO a wire contract rather than a reader's private struct. **Fixed in the port at Plan 9 Task 7 — the `OutlineJson.clearance` half only, and it is wired rather than dropped.** `readBoard:310`'s hard-coded `outlineClearanceNo = 1` becomes `outline_clearance_class(&clearance_matrix, outline.clearance, scale_factor)` (`crates/fr-dsn/src/kicad/reader.rs`): `clearance` is a distance in the document's units, which is exactly what `crate::kicad::writer` writes for the outline's own class (`get_value(class, class, 0, false) / scale_factor`), so the mapping back is scale-and-match and the round trip closes. It answers the **first** class whose diagonal equals the value, so `"default"` wins a tie; a `clearance` that is absent, zero or matches no class keeps Java's `1`. It deliberately does not create a class (a KiCad board's classes are the ones its `netClasses` declare) and does not round to the nearest one (a value the reader cannot map is one it does not understand). **Dropping it was rejected**: survey §9.1 forbids turning the DTO into the reader's private struct, and the field is a real design rule the writer already round-trips. The other three §9.1 fields keep their `keep` verdict and are untouched. Directed tests `crates/fr-dsn/tests/kicad_round_trip.rs::{the_outline_clearance_round_trips, an_outline_clearance_matching_no_class_keeps_the_default, an_absent_outline_clearance_keeps_the_default, the_other_three_dto_fields_still_round_trip}`. **Golden movement: none, and for two different reasons — both checked rather than assumed.** Five of the seven KiCad fixtures write `"clearance": 0.5` on a board whose classes are 2000 and (where there is a second) 2540 or 3000 internal units, so 5000 matches nothing and they keep Java's `1` by the fallback. The other two, the `*_output_session` pair, write a clearance that **does** match: `Issue368-CorneyIslandWireless_output_session.json`'s `0.2` is 2000, which is class 1's own value, and `Issue733-kicad_complex_hierarchy_output_session.json`'s `300.0` at `UM`/`10` is 3000, which is class 1's *and* class 2's — a value tie the ascending search resolves to class 1. So those two reach `1` by **matching the default**, not by failing to match; the answer is the same and the reason is not, which is why it is written out here. The two dead locals (`specctraLayerStructure`, `maxNetNo`) keep their `// not ported:` markers and are not this task's. | Delete the two locals. `OutlineJson.clearance` is the interesting one: it looks like an intended feature (`:310`'s literal `1` with the comment "Default clearance class"), so either wire it up or drop the field. | fixed: T7 in part (keep in part — §9.1: the four DTO fields are a **wire contract**, not dead code, and Plan 9 may not stop them round-tripping. `OutlineJson.clearance` was the FIX half, owed at **Task 7** and delivered there: it is wired. `NetClassJson.netNames`, `NetJson.id` and `LayerJson.index` stay `keep` and go on round-tripping unread. The two dead locals `specctraLayerStructure` and `maxNetNo` stay `// not ported:`, which is what keeps the row's mechanism true.) |
| 282 | A `null` `name` on a layer or a net class is stored and only crashes later, three hundred lines away (io/kicad/KiCadJsonReader.java:114, :131) | `boardLayers[i] = new Layer(layerJson.name, isSignal)` and `clearanceClassNames[i + 2] = additionalNetClasses.get(i).name` both take the DTO's `String` verbatim, `null` included — Gson sets a reference field to `null` for an explicit `"name": null`. Nothing in sections 1-8 dereferences either: `nonDefaultNetClasses` and `findKiCadDefaultNetClass` go through `KiCadNetClassNames.isKiCadDefaultNetClassName`, which null-checks (io/KiCadNetClassNames.java:26-28), and `ClearanceMatrix.getNo(name)` compares `row[i].name.equalsIgnoreCase(name)` — which throws only if the **row's** name is null, i.e. only when a custom clearance rule is also present. The first unconditional dereference is in **section 9**, `boardLayers[li].name.equalsIgnoreCase(layerName)` (`:545`), and only for a board that also has pads. So a hand-written JSON with a null layer name loads fine and dies on the components. | `// totalized:` at both sites in `crates/fr-dsn/src/kicad/reader.rs`: `fr_board::Layer::new` and `ClearanceMatrix::new` take a `String`, so the port stores `""` where Java stores `null`. Listed again under `totalized` below. The divergence is invisible on every real input — a KiCad export always writes the key, and all seven corpus fixtures do — and becomes visible only on a hand-written file, where Java crashes in section 9 and the port compares against the empty string. **Task 9 revisited this row while porting `:545`, and the claim holds — but it is both narrower and wider than this row originally said.** *As stated*: stem `layer-null-name-with-pads` (a two-layer board whose layer 0 has `"name": null` and whose one pad carries `"layers": ["B.Cu"]`) answers `ParseError location=json_payload detail=Exception occurred: Cannot invoke "String.equalsIgnoreCase(String)" because "boardLayers[li].name" is null` — `:545`, exactly as predicted. *Narrower*: `:545` fires only when the pad's `layers` list is **non-empty**, because `:539`'s `pad.layers != null && !pad.layers.isEmpty()` guard skips the whole comparison loop — stem `layer-null-name-no-pad-layers`, the same board with `"layers": []`, **loads**. *Wider*: a `null` **net** name never reaches section 9 at all. `Nets.get(String, int)` walks `currentNet.name.equalsIgnoreCase(name)` over the *stored* names (Nets.java:44), so section 8's auto-registration loop at `:491` already dereferences it whenever anything references a net; only a board that references none defers the same throw to section 9's `:639`. Both answer `Cannot invoke "String.equalsIgnoreCase(String)" because "currentNet.name" is null` — stems `net-null-name-with-pads` and `net-null-name-pad-without-net`. That throw is reproduced too: `reader.rs` keeps a `net_name_is_null: Vec<bool>` beside the totalized names and `java_nets_get` is `Nets.get` **including its NPE**, so it fires in walk order exactly as Java's does (a match earlier in the list returns before ever touching the null-named net). `crates/fr-dsn/src/kicad/reader.rs:545` carries the early return that reproduces the `:545` throw and cites this row. All four cases — the two layer ones and the two net ones — are pinned by `a_null_layer_name_crashes_in_section_9_exactly_where_quirk_282_says`, and all four stems are in `crates/fr-dsn/tests/data/p8t8-kicad-read-b.txt`, compared row by row by `the_whole_section_9_to_11_item_graph_matches_the_jvm`. A `null` component `reference` is a fourth stored name Gson can leave null on this path; it has its own row, **#287**. **Fixed in the port at Plan 9 Task 7.** `KiCadBoardJson::validate` (`crates/fr-dsn/src/kicad/dto.rs`) refuses a `null` name at the DTO boundary — a layer's, a net class's, a net's, a component's `reference` and a pad's — before `readBoard` reads anything, with a diagnostic naming **the file, the section and the object**: ``the KiCad board JSON file is malformed in its `layers` section: layers[1].name is null, and a board layer must have a name``, and the `ParseError`'s `location` is the section. The register's own suggestion, taking the reject arm. That deletes three side tables the port needed to reproduce the crashes — `board_layer_names`' `Option`, `net_name_is_null` and `package_pin_names` — makes `java_nets_get` total (no `Result`, no `name_is_null` parameter), and leaves `JavaNpe` dead (`// dead: killed by #282/#287, deleted at T24`). **What it does not refuse**: a `null` *list* (`"layers": null`), which is Java's unguarded dereference at `:105` and keeps its own message, and a `null` on a field where `null` means *absent* — `netName`, `className`, `value`, `layer`, `shape`, `classA`/`classB` — because a KiCad export writes those keys only when it has something to say. Directed tests `crates/fr-dsn/tests/kicad_dto.rs`, eight of them, four asserting the diagnostic **text** and two asserting the two non-refusals. **No corpus fixture carries a null name**: all seven were scanned, 0 hits over layers, net classes, nets, component references, pad names and pad layer elements. | Default the name to `""` (or reject it) at the DTO boundary, so the failure is at the file and not 400 lines downstream. | fixed: T7 |

The three sites where Java stores the `null` and then throws (`trace-point-null-element`, `outline-corner-null-element`, `netclass-null-element`) are unchanged and stay recorded divergences-in-prose: both sides reject the same file at the same `location`. | 284 | `getDescriptivePadstackName` generates a padstack key that encodes **neither the layer span nor the drill**, and `Padstacks.get` matches it case-insensitively, so the second pad to ask for a name silently inherits the first pad's shapes (io/kicad/KiCadJsonReader.java:560-564, :857-890; core/library/Padstacks.java:25-32) | The name is `<Shape>[<T\|B\|A>]Pad_<size.x*1000>[x<size.y*1000>]_um`, and `layerType` is `A` for **every** pad whose `layers` list is not exactly one entry long *and* for a single-entry list naming a layer that is neither the first nor the last. `:561`'s `padstacks.get(padstackName)` then returns whatever was registered under that key and `:562-564` skips the `add` — the `ConvexShape[] shapes` array built at `:508-557` is thrown away. Measured on stem `pad-layer-selection`, a three-layer board whose one component has six pads all of size 1x1: `span` (`layers: ["B.Cu","F.Cu"]`) registers `Rect[A]Pad_1000x1000_um` spanning layers 0-2; `mid` (`layers: ["In1.Cu"]`, a middle layer, so `layerType` is `A`) asks for the same name and gets `span`'s **three-layer** padstack instead of its own one-layer one; `none` (`layers: ["Nope.Cu"]`, matching nothing) gets it too. `Round` names drop `size.y` entirely (`:883`), so a 1x2 circle and a 1x5 circle share a padstack. The drill is likewise absent: `isDrillable` (`:559`) only reaches `padstacks.add`, so a drilled and an undrilled pad of the same size and layer type share one padstack and the **first** one's `attachAllowed`. | Reproduced at `crates/fr-dsn/src/kicad/reader.rs`'s `:561-564` site, whose `get_by_name` is `Padstacks.get`'s case-insensitive lookup, with the collision named at the site. `pad-layer-selection`, `pad-shape-arms` and `pad-name-half-up` pin it in `crates/fr-dsn/tests/data/p8t8-kicad-read-b.txt`, and `the_generated_padstack_names_match_the_jar` pins the name grammar itself. **Fixed in the port at Plan 9 Task 7, together with #286 — which it masked.** A pad's padstack is keyed on its **shape array** (which carries the layer set: a layer the pad is not on has no shape) and its **drill**, through a `pad_padstacks` index built as section 9 runs; the generated name is a display artefact from there on. The register's own second suggestion. Two consequences are deliberate and both measured. (a) Section 8's **via** padstacks are not in the index: a through-hole pad whose box happens to match `defaultVia`'s must not become a via padstack, which is the one merge Java's name lookup could never make and a structural one could. (b) Two structurally different padstacks can now generate the same name (`[A]` covers "every layer" and "one middle layer" alike, and the name carries no drill), so `unique_padstack_name` appends `#2`, `#3`, … — `#` and not the package ladder's `::`, because `Padstack::drill_radius` parses a name containing `:` for its `<outer>:<drill>_` fragment. **Measured on all seven KiCad fixtures: zero name collisions**, so no corpus board takes a suffix. `getDescriptivePadstackName` changes too, and this is the half that reaches the SES: `:882`'s `"Round".equals(shapeStr)` selected a one-number `Pad_<x>_um` form that dropped `size.y` — so a `1 x 2` and a `1 x 1` round pad had the same name — and every shape now takes the two-number form. `:885-888`'s `%.0fxf_%.0f` + `.replace("xf_", "x")` round trip goes with it (`String.replace` replaces *every* occurrence, so a shape name containing `xf_` was rewritten); the format string writes the `x` directly. The **via** name is untouched, deliberately: `Via[<s>-<e>]_<dia>:<drill>_um` already identifies its padstack, and `drill_radius` parses it. | 291 | `items.drill_item_count` is **always 0**, because `instanceof Pin` is tested before `instanceof DrillItem` and `Pin` *is* a `DrillItem` (core/scoring/BoardStatistics.java:161-173) | The classification chain at `:161-173` is `Trace`, `Via`, `ConductionArea`, `Pin`, **`DrillItem`**, `ComponentOutline`, `else`. `DrillItem` has exactly two concrete subclasses in the whole tree — `board/model/items/Pin.java` and `board/model/items/Via.java`, both `extends DrillItem` — and both are matched *above* it, so `:169-170`'s `this.items.drillItemCount++` is **unreachable on every board that has ever existed**. | 292 | The MCP stdio bridge strips **every** `\r` and `\n` from the response body before printing it (Freerouting.java:770-772) | `String singleLineResponse = responseBody.replace("\r", "").replace("\n", "");` then `originalOut.println(singleLineResponse)`. The intent is right — MCP's stdio framing is one JSON value per line, so an embedded newline would desynchronise the peer — but `replace` is applied to the **whole body**, not to the framing, and JSON string content may legally contain `\r`/`\n`… as the two-character escapes `\\r`/`\\n`, which are unaffected, **or as raw bytes, which Gson's `Strictness.LENIENT` writer never emits but an HTTP proxy, a pretty-printer or a future non-Gson writer may**. The moment one does, the bridge deletes the byte and hands the peer a *different, still-parseable* document rather than a framing error: a tool result whose text lost its line breaks, silently. The same line is also why the bridge cannot ever speak a pretty-printed dialect, and it does the work unconditionally on every response, including the 99.9 % that contain no such byte. Plan 8 label **M**, allocated by Task 14 — Task 11 recorded the divergence in code and did not claim an id. | **Not reproduced** (documented delta). `crates/freerouting/src/mcp/stdio.rs`'s `write_line` writes the serialised value followed by exactly one `\n` and strips nothing, because `serde_json::to_string` cannot emit a raw control character inside a string (it escapes them as `\\u000a`/`\\u000d`), so the framing is safe by construction rather than by deletion. The transport table in that file's module docs carries the row, and **delta row 5** of `crates/freerouting/README.md` — the *response framing* row, which cites this quirk back — records it as a deliberate divergence. (This cell read "row 8" until the final whole-branch review; row 8 is the blank-line-for-a-notification delta, a different thing. `crates/freerouting/src/mcp/server.rs`'s `write_line` already said "the fifth Task 11 row", so the register was the only place that disagreed.) | Frame, do not filter. Serialise compactly, assert the result contains no raw newline, and fail loudly if it ever does — deleting a byte out of a payload to protect a framing invariant hides the bug that broke it. | pinned |

| 294 | The micro-neckdown fanout fallback ignores the board's minimum track width (first bad commit `f31a0c84`, "Add micro-neckdown fanout fallback for trace insertion", shipped in v2.3.0) | A failed 2-point fanout insertion is retried at a **`LinkedHashSet` of up to five candidate half-widths** (`autoroute/path/FoundConnectionInserter.java:461-471`, re-read at Task 0): the start pin's `getTraceNeckdownHalfwidth(layer)`, the end pin's, then `max(1, base*3/4)`, `max(1, base*3/5)` and `max(1, base/2)` of `ctrl.traceHalfWidth[layer]`. The loop skips a candidate that is `<= 0` or `>= baseHalfWidth` and keeps the same clearance class, and there is **no check against `min_track_width` anywhere on the path**. (The report's prose says "the pin's neckdown width, then 75 % and then 50 %"; that is the same mechanism with the `3/5` rung and the second pin elided — this row is the one Task 2 reads, so it carries the code's five.) On any board whose net-class width equals its minimum width — very common — the fallback emits sub-minimum traces: `track_width` violations on 31/146 of the report's small-tier boards, DRC-clean 0.94 → 0.73 (small) and 0.93 → 0.40 (large). It recovers **no** connectivity on the small tier (0.76 with or without it); its benefit is real only on large boards (fully-connected 0.17 → 0.33). The opt-in `neck_width_um` retry from `9b782275` is off by default and is **not** involved | **Fixed in the port at Plan 9 Task 2 (R2).** `crates/fr-router/src/autoroute/path/inserter.rs::insert_fanout_micro_neckdown` skips any candidate half width below `BoardRules::get_min_trace_half_width()` (`crates/fr-board/src/rules/board_rules.rs:138`, the minimum over the **declared** net-class widths) — **not** `Board::get_min_trace_half_width()` (`crates/fr-board/src/board/mod.rs:1787`), which is a running minimum over the traces already inserted and would ratchet itself down. The `// Java bug:` / `// fixed: T2 (#294)` pair sits at the loop guard. When the class width already is the minimum the whole fallback is skipped and the connection fails honestly. Tests, in that file's own `#[cfg(test)]` module because the method is private: `the_micro_neckdown_fallback_never_goes_below_the_rules_minimum` (fails before), `the_fallback_still_necks_down_when_the_class_is_above_the_minimum` (the large-board benefit preserved — the `3/4` rung is still taken) and `the_guard_reads_the_rules_minimum_not_the_running_board_minimum` (fails before) | **Java-side fix owed** (recommendation 8's standing list, PR 2 of 2): a **guard, not a revert** — skip any retry width below the board's minimum track width (or the class width when the class is already at the minimum). Evidence: `benchmark/reports/java-regressions-2026-09.md` §"Regression 2", offending commit `f31a0c84` | fixed: T2, accepted: M1 (BV) |
| 295 | The every-4th-pass exhaustive ("slow") search tree, removed in the same commit as #293 (`933d2980`) | `useSlowAlgorithm = p_pass_no % 4 == 0` became a constant `false`, so the exhaustive search tree that previously ran on every fourth pass never runs. Commit `933d2980` removed **two** behaviours at once; #293 is the first and this is the second, and the report's ablation restores only the first — so this one's contribution is **not** separately measured and its row says so | **Investigation, not a fix — opened by Plan 9 Task 2 (I1) and owned by Task 8.** The port carries the flag's remains in the same shape Java does: `crates/fr-board/src/rules/board_rules.rs:61` (the field), `:514` and `:519` (the two accessors), with **no reader** — `grep -rn use_slow_autoroute_algorithm crates` answers those three lines and nothing else. Task 2 measured #293's sort on its own and did **not** restore the slow tree with it, so this row's contribution is still unmeasured. **The open question:** the "slow" tree is the base `ShapeSearchTree`, i.e. the one whose `completeShape` keeps the room the 90-degree override drops (**#159**), so an every-4th-pass slow tree was among other things a periodic workaround for #159 — and the measurement is only meaningful **after #159 lands, in Task 8**. Task 8's report answers it: if a periodic exhaustive tree still buys nothing once #159 is fixed, delete the flag and both accessors in the port and file the Java-side deletion. **Do not restore it blind**: it is a 4x cost on every 4th pass with no measured benefit **I1 ANSWERED at Plan 9 Task 8, and the answer is not the one the row expected — do not delete the flag.** The row asks: *"if a periodic exhaustive tree still buys nothing once #159 is fixed, delete the flag and both accessors in the port and file the Java-side deletion."* It buys something. **Method:** the "slow" tree is the base `ShapeSearchTree`, which `SearchTreeManager.getAutorouteTree:147-161` selects instead of the 45- or 90-degree subclass, so the measurement is a one-line swap at `crates/fr-board/src/searchtree/manager.rs` — `AngleRestriction::None` for the autoroute tree only, leaving `rules.trace_angle_restriction` alone — behind a throwaway `P9T8_SLOW_TREE` env var that is **not** committed. Six `-mp`-capped router stems, `FR_ROUTER_BUDGET=disabled`, incompletes and violations from the referee's own DRC document. **The measurement had to wait for #162, not only #159, and that is the first finding.** Taken before #162 landed, the slow tree was **SIGKILLed** on `router-j2-reference` (after 77 s) and `router-fanout-bm11` (after 121 s): the base tree's `completeShape` produces simplex rooms whose `toSimplex()` drops lines, which is #162's trigger exactly, so a pre-#162 measurement measures #162 and calls it #295. With #162 fixed every stem terminates. **The result, all nine of Task 8's fixes in place** (`normal` is the port's angle-restricted tree, `slow` the base one): `router-rpi-splitter` 0 -> 0 inc at 0.096 s -> 0.216 s; `router-j2-reference` **1 -> 0** at 1.102 s -> 0.907 s; `router-ecc83-input` 0 -> 0 at 0.034 s -> 0.053 s; `router-dac2020-bm01` **30 -> 29** at 18.0 s -> 101.8 s; `router-fanout-bm11` **7 -> 6** inc and **6 -> 4** violations at 3.15 s -> 24.3 s; `router-strict-drc-cnh` **14 -> 15** at 11.0 s -> 109.2 s. So: a small, real, **non-uniform** connectivity gain — three stems better by one connection, one worse by one, one stem's violations halved — at **2x to 10x** CPU. Two caveats on the numbers, both in the direction of over-statement: the probe swaps the tree on **every** pass where Java's flag was every **fourth**, so both the cost and the benefit are roughly 4x over-stated, and six stems is not a bench. **The #159 connection the row rests on is dismissed:** #159 is a 90-degree-only defect and every corpus board declares `fortyfive_degree`, so the slow tree was never a workaround for it *here*; what the base tree buys is its own `restrainShape` geometry. **Recommendation, replacing the row's improvement column:** keep `useSlowAlgorithm` and both accessors — the premise for deleting them ("no measured benefit") is now false — and put the every-4th-pass form to a **milestone bench** (M1/M2/M3, 605 boards) before deciding, since a 25-125 % CPU cost for a fraction of a connection per board is a trade only a bench can adjudicate. Status stays `candidate`; the investigation is closed and the decision is a controller/bench one | **Java-side fix owed**: restore the periodic exhaustive pass, or record why it is not worth restoring once #293's ablation is in. Evidence: `benchmark/reports/java-regressions-2026-09.md` §"Regression 1" item 2, offending commit `933d2980` | candidate (I1 answered: T8) |

The "recovery" work that followed the #293 ordering regression appears to try harder with more vias, so this may be a *consequence* of #293 rather than an independent defect — which is why it is a **watched column** at every Plan 9 milestone (G2's via row, ruling BO) rather than a fix with a sketch | **Watched column, opened by Plan 9 Task 2 (I2) and owned jointly by M1 and Task 18.** Via count is a first-class G2 metric, so it is measured either way: `benchmark/baselines/ab/quality-ab-T2.tsv`'s `via_total` / `via_through` / `via_blind` / `via_buried` columns carry it per stem at this task's head, and the corpus figure lands in `benchmark/reports/java-regressions-2026-09.md`'s status appendix at M1 against the `v1.0.0-rs` run of record. **The open question:** if the inflation is the #293 ordering regression's downstream compensation — the "recovery" work trying harder with more vias — then restoring the sort deflates it on its own and there is no second defect. If it **survives** #293, bisect v2.2.4 -> v2.3.0 with the suite, and read the answer together with **#172** (a 10x via discount on pure-SMD nets, Task 18), which is plausibly the same phenomenon from the other end. **M1 answers the open question: the inflation SURVIVES #293 + #294.** Corpus totals over the 605 `pcbench` boards, `benchmark/reports/plan9-m1-vs-java.json` against `benchmark/reports/v1.0.0-vs-java-clean.json`, both against the frozen `java-278fe14` view: jar **15 402** vias, `v1.0.0-rs` (the pre-fix port) **15 539** (1.0089x the jar), `plan9-m1` (post-R1/R2) **16 129** (1.0472x the jar) — **+590 vias, +3.80 % against the port's own pre-fix position**, on 214 boards up / 172 down / 219 unchanged. Per D3 subset, `plan9-m1` over `v1.0.0-rs`: d3-a **+1.72 %** (407 -> 414), d3-b **+2.44 %** (2 947 -> 3 019), d3-c **+4.19 %** (12 185 -> 12 696). The `via_ratio` against the human reference moves 0.9636 -> 0.9807 mean (the jar's is 0.9700), median 0.6667 unchanged. **Read the 29-stem A/B beside it and do not confuse the two scales**: `benchmark/baselines/ab/quality-ab-T2.tsv` measured vias **down 4.6 %** over its 29 stems, which is a real result about those stems and not about the corpus | **Java-side fix owed**, and the diagnosis is now due rather than conditional: M1 measured the inflation surviving #293 and #294 (see the middle column), so it is **its own defect** and the next step is the sketch's second branch — bisect v2.2.4 -> v2.3.0 with the suite and read the answer together with **#172** (a 10x via discount on pure-SMD nets, Task 18). Evidence: `benchmark/reports/java-regressions-2026-09.md` §"Secondary findings", plus the M1 corpus figures above | candidate (corpus +3.80 % vs the pre-fix port at M1, survives R1+R2 — bisect due at T18, watch at M2) |
| 297 | `MazeSearchEngine.reduceTraceShapesAtTiePins`'s tie-pin / foreign-trace predicate (autoroute/maze/MazeSearchEngine.java:157-162) never fires on the Plan 9 corpus — surfaced by Task 17's #193 measurement, not a #193 finding itself | The method is the *only* board write inside the maze search (`init:970-971`, before any room or door exists): for each item in the caller's start/destination set it asks `currentItem instanceof Pin currentTiePin && currentItem.netCount() > 1` (`:157`), then for each of that pin's `getNormalContacts()` neighbours asks whether it is a `Trace` on a foreign net (`:160-162`), and reduces that trace's tree shape so the tie pin's centre stays reachable. Two readings, undecided: **(a)** no corpus board has a multi-net tie pin with a foreign-net trace actually touching it, or **(b)** the predicate is mis-ported and the guard is dead by defect rather than by absence — Task 17's isolated worktree had the measurement but not the means to tell the two apart. | **Investigation, not a fix — opened by Plan 9 Task 17 (`docs/plan-9-prep/stale-index-report.md` §6 item 3, ruling BY) and owned by Task 10** (shove/maze territory). Four of the five are search-graph invariants (every edge is an edge line of both its neighbours; the constructor always inserts an anchor). **`positionLocate` is not**: the bounding triangle's hypotenuse is `x + y == 2^25`, so a corner like `(2e7, 2e7)` — inside `CRIT_INT` on each axis *separately* — is outside it and Java genuinely throws. What makes it unreachable is upstream, in the DSN reader: `io/specctra/parser/Structure.java:1200`, `while (5 * maxCoor >= Limits.CRIT_INT) { scaleFactor /= 10; maxCoor /= 10; }`, bounds every imported coordinate to about `CRIT_INT / 5` per axis, so no two of them can sum to `2^25`. **Caveat:** a `Board` built programmatically rather than imported has no such guard — there a release build silently drops the corner where Java throws, and a debug build trips the `debug_assert!` | `crates/fr-board/src/datastructures/delaunay.rs`, six `// totalized:` markers | totalized |

All but the row above are **unreachable defensive arms** — a `null` `boundingOctagon`, an out-of-range `polyline.lines[i + 1]`, a `Polyline` constructor that cannot receive fewer than three lines from its caller — and under the §Process-notes rule they stay comments at the site rather than rows here. `grep -rn "// totalized:" crates/*/src` is the complete list; this table is the subset a reader must be told about.)*

## Improvement candidates (`candidate`) — not Java bugs

| Idea | Why | Where | status |
|---|---|---|---|
| Replace `BigInteger` fallback with `i128` where products provably fit | Java promotes to `BigInteger` above 2^25; most intermediate products fit in `i128` — big speed win in `Line.intersection`, `perpendicularProjection`. | `line.rs`, `int_point.rs`, `rational_*` | candidate |
| Lawful `Ord` on `IntDirection`/`Line` once NULL is defined as minimum | Enables `BTreeMap`/`sort` without `sort_by` shims. | see #1, #6 | candidate |
| `pub(crate)` fields on `RationalPoint`/`BigIntDirection` | Java package-private; guards the `z >= 0` invariant. | `rational_point.rs` | candidate |
| Rename `RationalVector::determinant` → `numerator_determinant` | Ignores denominators; sign-correct only. | `rational_vector.rs` | candidate |
| Add `Hash` to `Vector`/`Direction` when a map key is needed | Java has no `hashCode` there; add only on demand. | `vector.rs`, `direction.rs` | candidate |
| `Display` for `FloatPoint` uses exact binary expansion, Java uses shortest-round-trip digits | Diverges above 2^53 and at 4th-digit ties; diagnostic only. | `float_point.rs` | candidate |
| Drop `precalculated*` memo fields (already done) | Keeps geometry types `Copy`/`Eq`/`Hash`; recompute is cheap. In `Polyline` the memo is also *observably* equivalent: the `Polyline(Line[])` constructor fills it before flipping a line, and `intersectionApprox` negates numerator and denominator together, which is exact. | `simplex.rs`, `line_segment.rs`, `polyline.rs` | candidate |
| ~~Memo cache for `PolygonShape` convex pieces / `PolylineArea` split~~ — **discharged in Plan 2 Task 10.** Java memoizes in `PolygonShape.precalculatedConvexPieces` (PolygonShape.java:17) / `PolylineArea.precalculatedConvexPieces` (PolylineArea.java:31); `fr-geometry` still recomputes per call, deliberately (quirk #30 made the divider's `Random` per-call so the result is reproducible under concurrency), so the memo moved up to the *item* level instead: `ObstacleAreaData::convex_pieces` and `BoardOutline::keepout_convex_pieces`, both `OnceLock<Option<Vec<TileShape>>>`, emptied at exactly the invalidation points that empty the absolute-area memo. `ComponentOutline` deliberately gets none — nothing splits one (`tileShapeCount` is `return 0`). | Correctness parity was never affected (recompute gives the same pieces); the memo restores Java's amortised cost for `tileShapeCount`/`getTileShape`/`calculateTreeShapes`, the last of which splits the outline keepout once per layer. | `crates/fr-board/src/items/area.rs`, `crates/fr-board/src/structure/board_outline.rs` | candidate |
| **Ladder hang in DSN import.** — **fully discharged: Plan 3 Task 10, Plan 6 Task 10b and Plan 8 Task 3** (the re-point from Plan 6/7 to Plan 8 is a **controller ruling**, accepted in Task 10b's review: the seam is built and what is left is a DSN-reader change, not a router one). Quirk #76: a two-rail, four-rung ladder on one net makes the import normalisation non-terminating, and neither normalisation cap reaches it. `io/specctra/parser/Wiring.java:347` ends every DSN read with `board.normalizeAllTraces()`, so **any imported design containing that pattern hangs the reader** — in Java today. Plan 3 took ruling 4's option for *that call*: run it under a `TimeLimit`-backed `StopCheck` (`DsnReadOptions::normalize_time_limit`, default 60 s) rather than bounding `split`'s walk, so every design that terminates normalises identically and one that does not produces Java's own `"Wiring: normalization of traces failed"` warning (Wiring.java:349) instead of a wedged process. The check is consulted in `normalize_all_traces_checked`/`normalize_traces_checked`'s passes, in `normalize_trace_checked`'s recursion, at the head of `split_trace_checked`'s entry walk and — the one ruling 4 did not anticipate, and the one that actually matters — inside `connection_items_checked`'s walk along the contacts (see quirk #106). **The seam is built (Plan 6 ruling 6, Task 10b); the `fr-dsn` wiring is the last step.** `Wiring.readViaScope`'s `board.insertVia` (Wiring.java:706) reaches the *same* machinery by a second route — `BasicBoard.insertVia` walks `fromLayer..toLayer` calling `splitTraces` -> `PolylineTrace.split` (BasicBoard.java:287-293) — and it sits **outside** the `try`/`catch` Java wraps `normalizeAllTraces` in, so ruling 4's `StopCheck` does not reach it. Plan 6 Task 10b added `Board::insert_via_checked`, `Board::insert_escape_via_checked` and `Board::split_traces_checked`, keeping the three original names as delegating `|| false` wrappers, because `ForcedViaInserter::insert` needed exactly that (plan-3 ruling F, now discharged in `docs/plan-3-handoff.md`); `crates/fr-router/tests/forced_via.rs` `insert_stops_when_the_stop_check_trips` pins a tripping check on a four-rung ladder answering `BoardError::Stopped`. **The last line landed in Plan 8 Task 3**: `read_via_scope` now calls `Board::insert_via_checked` with a `TimeLimit`-backed stop built from `p.options.normalize_time_limit_ms()`, from the same `DsnReadOptions::normalize_time_limit` `read_wiring_scope` gives `normalize_all_traces_checked` at `wiring.rs:78-84`, with the `limit_ms <= 0` test **inverted**: at the normalisation site a trip lands in Java's own `catch` and costs one warning, so `Duration::ZERO` can mean "skip normalisation deterministically"; at the via site Java has no `catch`, so a stop can only fail the read, and a non-positive budget therefore means *no bound at this site* — which is exactly Java. `crates/fr-dsn/tests/dsn_reader.rs::a_normalisation_time_limit_trip_produces_javas_own_warning` is the pin that makes the difference load-bearing: it passes `Duration::ZERO` and still expects a `Success`. The budget is **per via**, not shared across the wiring scope: Java has no budget here at all, so a shared one would stop reading a large board's later vias for a reason Java has no counterpart for. Task 3 re-ran the gate the re-point existed for — `cargo test -p fr-dsn`, `scripts/differential/sweep-p3t15.sh` at 525 MATCH + 5 XDIFF, `scripts/differential/run.sh p6t1` on the five Plan 6 stems and `cargo test -p fr-router --test batch_parity` — all unchanged. | A hang has no output to be parity-checked against, so the usual "reproduce it" rule gave no guidance; the decision was made by the plan that first calls `normalizeAllTraces` on user input. Safety net for the discharged half: `every_fixture_in_the_corpus_matches_javas_result_and_warnings` asserts that **no** fixture in the corpus trips the limit. | `crates/fr-board/src/board/{trace_normalize,normalize,connectivity,query}.rs`, `crates/fr-dsn/src/parser/wiring.rs` (the `insert_via_checked` call and the comment that replaced the `// obligation:` marker), `crates/fr-board/tests/trace_normalize.rs`, `crates/fr-dsn/tests/dsn_reader.rs` | candidate |
| ~~**MCP concurrency (Plan 8 obligation).**~~ — **DISCHARGED in Plan 8 Task 11.** `ToolHandler` is now `Box<dyn Fn(&State, Value, &ProgressWriter, &CancelToken) -> Result<Value, RpcError> + Send + Sync>`; `mcp::server::ProgressWriter` writes `notifications/progress` through the shared writer and **drops everything** when the request carried no `_meta.progressToken`, so a tool never branches; and `run_with` is plan ruling 3's shape exactly — a reader thread feeding an `mpsc` channel, the main thread dispatching and owning the in-flight map, one tool thread per outstanding `tools/call`, and an `Arc<Mutex<W>>` writer held across write-and-flush. An inbound `notifications/cancelled` looks its `requestId` up in that map and calls `CancelToken::cancel()` **while** the tool runs, which `a_cancelled_tool_stops_mid_flight` pins by refusing to close stdin until the tool has reported that it saw the flag. Ruling 4's `catch_unwind` sits at exactly one site, the handler invocation, and renders the payload into `RpcError::internal` (`-32603`); `a_panicking_tool_becomes_an_error_and_the_server_survives` pins that the next request is still answered. The obligation's own sketch said `&mut State`; what landed is `&State` with the one mutable cell an `AtomicBool`, because a `&mut` across an `Arc<State>` shared with the main loop would be a lock held for a whole routing run — the thing ruling AP tells the port not to build. **The `fr-router` poll seam (controller ruling BB) landed with it**: `RouterStop::{with_cancel_poll, poll_cancel}`, three loop heads, `None` on every parity run. Original text follows.** `mcp/server.rs`'s `ToolHandler = Box<dyn Fn(Value) -> Result<Value, RpcError> + Send>` and `handle -> Option<Response>` can emit exactly one message and hold no connection reference, and `mcp/stdio.rs::run_with` is a strictly sequential read-handle-write loop. Spec §13 requires outbound `notifications/progress` during routing and inbound `notifications/cancelled` → `CancelToken`; neither is expressible. Plan 8 must give the handler `&mut State` plus a progress sink, let `handle` emit several messages per request, and split `run_with` into a reader thread feeding a channel with a `Mutex`-guarded writer, so a cancellation arriving mid-tool can flip that tool's `CancelToken`. | Not a Java quirk — a Plan 1 scaffolding limit deliberately left in place: the redesign is only testable once a tool runs long enough to report progress. Recorded so Plan 8 does not discover it after registering the real tools. Doc comments on both items carry the same note (`// Plan 8 obligation:`) — **rewritten in Task 11 to record what landed**. | `crates/freerouting/src/mcp/server.rs`, `crates/freerouting/src/mcp/stdio.rs`, `crates/freerouting/src/mcp/jsonrpc.rs`, `crates/freerouting/tests/mcp_stdio.rs`, `crates/freerouting/README.md` (the delta table: ten message-shape rows plus row 11's shutdown timing), `crates/fr-router/src/pipeline/stop.rs` (the poll seam), `crates/fr-core/src/cancel.rs` (`apply_to`'s discharge note) | candidate |
| **Legacy CLI value normalisation — the `fr-settings` half is DISCHARGED (Plan 4 Task 7, `1c81176`); the wiring half is OPEN, Plan 8.** `legacy.rs` only re-spells flags; it forwards every value raw. Java normalises while parsing (`GlobalSettings.java:675-731`) and again in `RouterSettings.validate()`/`normalizeMaxThreads`: `-oit` is divided by 100 then floored at 0, `-mp` clamps to 1..9999 with **0 meaning unlimited**, `-mt` clamps to 0..1024 and — contrary to the obvious reading — `0` selects the *single-threaded* optimizer on the CLI path — though row 143 shows the headless path selects it regardless, because `BatchOptimizer.createForHeadless` (`:51-53`) never reads the field and the `> 1` gate at `:58` is inside the GUI factory, `-us`/`-is` fall back to `GREEDY`/`PRIORITIZED` for any unrecognised word, and `-inc` does not trim its comma-separated entries. Every rule is tabulated in `docs/cli-legacy-flags.md`. **What Plan 4 did:** `crates/fr-settings/src/sources/cli.rs` carries the whole table — `apply_command_line_arguments` → `LegacyBridge` (dead by plan 4 ruling 8, quirk #131), `CliSettings` for the two flags that reach the router, and `classify_de_arguments` for the `-de` rule — JVM-verified against the clone's HEAD jar with **one correction to the table**: `-oit -5` does *not* clamp to `0.0f`, it keeps the previous value, because the blanket rule never consumes an argument starting with `-` (quirk #135, `docs/cli-legacy-flags.md` updated). **Still open (Plan 8):** plan 4 ruling 10 left `crates/freerouting` untouched, so `legacy.rs` still forwards raw values and still reproduces the `-de` rule itself; Plan 8 rewires it to these two functions, and must also decide — as a recorded product decision, not a drift — whether to make the five dead flags live, which would make the port *more capable than Java*. | Derived once from the Java so Plans 5 and 8 do not re-derive it wrong; applying it in the shim would double-apply it (or apply it only on the legacy path and not the native one). | `docs/cli-legacy-flags.md`; `crates/fr-settings/src/sources/cli.rs` (`apply_command_line_arguments`, `CliSettings`, `classify_de_arguments`); `crates/freerouting/src/legacy.rs` (module doc); `docs/plan-4-handoff.md` | candidate |
| ~~**`ViaInfoId` renumbering across `ViaInfos.remove` (Plan 3 obligation).**~~ — **discharged in Plan 3 Task 14, and a second instance of the same hazard found and fixed with it. The *re-pointing* side-effect it exposed is re-filed as the open row directly below.** Java's `ViaRule` holds `ViaInfo` *object references* (ViaRule.java:21), so `ViaInfos.remove` (ViaInfos.java:62-64) can delete from the middle of the list without disturbing any rule. This port addresses via infos by index (`ViaInfoId`), so the same removal shifts every later index and silently re-points a rule's vias at the wrong via. The one non-GUI caller, `io/specctra/RulesReader.java:340-350` (`applyViaInfo`), removes a via only to add a replacement with the same name; it went through `BoardRules::replace_via_info_renumbering_rules`, which did the removal, the tail append and the renumbering in one step (`> old_id` decrements, `== old_id` becomes the new tail id). **Since Plan 7 Task 0 that method is `BoardRules::replace_via_info` and the renumbering half is gone**: `ViaRule` owns its `ViaInfo`s, so nothing stores a `ViaInfoId` across the removal (see the row below). The GUI's `gui/windows/routing/WindowEditVias.java:197` calls `remove` too, and stays out of scope. **The second instance:** `Network.addViaRule` (Network.java:414-416) removes a same-named `ViaRule` from `BoardRules.via_rules` before appending its replacement, and `NetClass::via_rule` is a `ViaRuleId` — Task 14 is the second caller (`RulesReader.applyViaRule`), the first to reach it after net classes hold indices, so `BoardRules::replace_via_rule_renumbering_net_classes` does the same job one level up. Caught by the Issue107 JVM golden, where `1A_EXTERNAL_1oz`'s net class otherwise silently acquired `Breiter`'s vias. | Not a Java quirk — a consequence of the Plan 2 "no object references between rules" design rule. `ViaInfos::remove`'s doc comment and `ids.rs`'s `ViaInfoId`/`ViaRuleId` carry the same note. ## KEEP (`keep`) — deliberate behaviour Plan 9 is forbidden to "fix" (survey §9.1)

With byte parity gone, "the jar does it" is no longer a reason for anything. These ten survive
that test on their own merits, and **no Plan 9 task may change any of them**. Where a row of the
numbered table above carries the same behaviour, its `status` cell says so; the three entries that
are not table rows live only here.

| # | what is kept | why it is load-bearing, not merely inherited | status |
|---|---|---|---|
| **#62** | The base `ShapeSearchTree` inflates a drill item's tree shape with `enlarge` and the 45-degree subclass with `offset`; on a diagonal they differ by √2 | **Not a bug.** The subclass's own comment gives the reason ("to avoid small corner cutoffs"). The register pins it because it is the single easiest thing to get wrong when collapsing the three tree classes into one — which Plan 9 may well attempt | keep |
| **#83** | `ClearanceMatrix.setValue` indexes J-then-I, and a KiCad-sourced board's clearance matrix is genuinely **asymmetric** because `KiCadJsonReader` writes only one order | Java is internally consistent; "fixing" the asymmetry by writing both orders would change the clearances of every KiCad board for no defect. The register's standing warning to the KiCad reader stands | keep |
| **#92** | The port's DSN/SES writer emits **2.3.0**'s snake_case Specctra tokens, not HEAD's camelCase | HEAD's own writer produces files **HEAD's own lexer cannot read back** (a re-read drops `host_cad`, every via rule, every per-type clearance rule and 26 wires). The port's spelling is the correct one, and it is what makes family G regenerable | keep |
| **#202's distinction** | The three-state stop (`NONE` / `AUTO_ROUTER_ONLY` / `ALL`) must not collapse to a bool | It is the difference between "stop routing" and "stop everything", and #202 + #227 both turn on it. `CancelToken` → `RouterStop` already preserves it. *(Row #202 itself is a Task 9 fix; only this distinction is kept.)* | keep |
| **#273's order** | The DRC path imports the session **after** re-parsing the `.rules` file | Measured both ways (structural hash 3938620220908954804 / 15 violations vs 945128192703554816 / 0); the rules-first board is **the correct one** — wires and vias should be created against the rules that govern them. Only the *asymmetry* with the router path's adjacent-rules probe is a fix | keep |
| **#281's four DTO fields** | `OutlineJson.clearance`, `NetClassJson.netNames`, `NetJson.id`, `LayerJson.index` are parsed and never read by `readBoard` | They are a **wire contract**: the KiCad writer has to write them back, which is what makes the DTO a document model rather than a reader's private struct. Plan 9 Task 7 settled `OutlineJson.clearance`'s separate FIX by **wiring** it — the reader maps its distance back to the clearance class the writer took it from — which is the branch that keeps this row true; dropping the field would have made the DTO the reader's private struct. **The other three keep `keep`**: they are still parsed, still unread by `readBoard`, and still written back unchanged, and `crates/fr-dsn/tests/kicad_round_trip.rs::the_other_three_dto_fields_still_round_trip` pins that. | keep |
| **`normalized_score` as `f32`** | Java's `float` association, three roundings in the penalty term, one narrowing in the cost term — `fr_router::score::normalized_score` | Both `AutorouteBatchLoop`'s `> lastBestScore + 0.5` and `BatchOptimizer`'s `< improvementThreshold` are **threshold comparisons** on it. Widening to `f64` for "cleaner" reporting changes which board a run keeps | keep |
| **#12, #14, #19, #20, #10, #8** | Six accepted divergences and "none needed" rows the register's own columns close | Re-checked against the no-parity rule: none has an observable consequence, and #8 (`31*a + b` with silent overflow) is fine **as a hash** — it is only wrong as an identity, which is #156's row, not this one | keep |
| **the `// Java bug:` markers** | 165 markers across 8 crates (`grep -rn '// Java bug:' crates`) | They are the provenance that makes this register navigable from the code. **Keep every one**, and add `// fixed: T<n>` beside it naming the Plan 9 task — do not delete the description of what the code used to do, because that is what a future reader compares the jar against | keep |
| **§8's KEEP shims** | `java_double_to_string`, the `java_round` family, `java_min`/`java_max`, `JavaNumberFormatter`, `java_format_fixed`, the name/enum contracts, `java_double_stream_sum` | Wire contracts, geometry rules, NaN-propagation semantics and a compensated sum — ~700 of the ~900 shim occurrences. Plan 9's Tasks 23/24 delete the *other* ~200 and none of these | keep |


## Process notes

- Reviews that extract the Java method bodies verbatim into a JDK-23 harness
  and diff against the Rust on random inputs have been the most effective
  verification (20k–525k comparisons per task). Prefer that over hand-tracing
  for any algorithm over ~100 lines.
- Every deliberate divergence must be greppable. The marker vocabulary, complete as
  of Plan 9 Task 0:

  | marker | means | set by |
  |---|---|---|
  | `// not ported:` | a member deliberately dropped (GUI, `FRLogger`, observers, serialization, a dead path) — the line names the Java member and the evidence | Plan 1 |
  | `// renamed:` | ported under another name, or moved to another crate; the line names both ends | Plan 2 |
  | `// totalized:` | Rust returns a value where Java crashes | Plan 1 |
  | `// Java bug:` | a reproduced defect, with the register row's number | Plan 1 |
  | `// added in Task N:` / `// added in Plan N:` | a deferral **with an owner**. `audit-port.sh` accepts it as satisfying a method, so it records an obligation rather than waiving a check | Plan 2 |
  | `// obligation:` | work a later task or plan must do that is *not* a Java member — a coverage gap, a measurement owed, a seam to build | Plan 3 |
  | `// pub seam:` | why a `pub` item is `pub` when nothing in the crate calls it. `rustc`'s `dead_code` lint is blind to an uncalled `pub` item in a library, so the line says what the Java declaration is and who the caller will be — or that there is no caller in Java either | Plan 6 (finding S7) |
  | `// not reachable:` | **Plan 7, controller ruling AJ.** Distinct from `not ported:`: the Java member *exists and is called*, but the call is guarded by a condition no live path satisfies, so porting it would be porting dead code. The line must name the guard. The five `RoutingBoard.additionalUpdateAfterChange` sites are the founding case — `retainAutorouteDatabase` is a Java benchmark-only system property | Plan 7 |
  | `// fixed: T<n> (#id)` | the port **no longer reproduces** the defect the neighbouring `// Java bug:` marker describes; the line names the Plan 9 task, **the register row's id in parentheses**, and in one clause what the port does instead. The id is not decoration: it is what lets `crates/fr-core/tests/register.rs::a_fixed_row_has_a_fixed_marker` check a row against **its own** marker rather than against any marker any task happened to write, and what lets the same test require **one marker per site** wherever the `// Java bug:` markers carry the id too. The `// Java bug:` marker beside it is **never** deleted — it is the provenance that makes this register navigable from the code, and it is what a future reader compares the jar against (survey §9.1) | Plan 9 |

- **The marker gate is two greps, not one** (plan-7 scan ruling 2, and the correction
  it made to plan-6 §10). A plan's closing gate is
  `grep -rn "added in Plan N" crates/*/src` **and** `crates/*/tests`, both empty —
  **not** `crates/`, which additionally sweeps the READMEs' prose *about* the
  convention and can never be driven to zero without deleting the explanation. A
  README may discuss a consumed marker, in the past tense; it may not read as a live
  one. Plan 7 closed with both gates empty and three past-tense README mentions.
- **Plan 7 controller ruling AI — the wall clock is a poll, and every parity run
  disables it on both sides.** Java gives a job a timeout through a monitor *thread*
  (`RoutingJobSchedulerActionThread.java:55-90`); the port has no watchdog and reads
  the deadline at exactly the points Java reads `job.state`. The six read sites are
  **two** mechanisms — one job-level flag and four per-stage clocks that write only
  their own `isTimedOut` — and flattening them would end stages Java leaves running.
  The table is `crates/fr-router/src/pipeline/stop.rs`'s module doc. Every `p7t*`
  driver passes `RouterBudget::disabled()` on the Rust side and puts
  `fanout.maxMillisecondsPerPin` / `optimizer.timeoutString` out of reach on the Java
  side; **a driver that cannot disable the budget is not a parity driver.** The one
  budget that cannot be disabled at all is quirk #234's inlined
  `TIME_LIMIT_TO_PREVENT_ENDLESS_LOOP`, and a MATCH against it is the evidence it
  never tripped.
- **Plan 7 controller ruling AH — hash parity is *decision* parity, not digest
  parity.** `Board::structural_hash` covers the field set `serialize(true)` covers,
  and the acceptance is that the three Java sites that compare two hashes
  (`BatchFanout:152-156`, `BoardHistory.contains`, `BoardHistory.getRank`) reach the
  same decisions over a scripted mutation sequence (`p7t10`, 0 diffs). Never
  reproduce the MD5 bytes. The row-per-field audit table is
  `crates/fr-board/src/board/snapshot.rs`'s module doc, and its five *skipped* rows
  are the only judgement calls in it.
- **Plan 7 controller ruling AK — one `ProgressSink`, and no port decision reads
  it.** `NamedAlgorithm`'s three listener lists and all six files of
  `autoroute/events/**` are replaced by a single `&mut dyn ProgressSink`. Plan-7
  ruling 11 makes it an observer in the strict sense; `a_recording_sink_changes_no_board_byte`
  is the pin. Plan 8 moves it to `fr-core` or re-exports it **unchanged**.
- **Plan 7 controller ruling BA — the optimizer's restore replays undo's *tree* side
  effects.** `BasicBoard.undo` restores `components` and `itemList` and then
  `applyUndoRedoSideEffects` (`:1255-1286`) removes every cancelled item and inserts
  every restored one **in the live search trees**. A whole-board clone assignment
  produces the same leaf *set* and a different tree *topology*, and
  `ShapeSearchTree45Degree.completeShape`'s shrinking-traversal prune makes topology
  decide which obstacles restrain a room — so the two sides burn different item ids
  from the first `improved=false` item on (quirk #229, localised by Task 14b, fixed by
  Task 14c). The port keeps ruling 8's clone for the **item state** and ports the
  **side effects** as `Board::{begin_undo_journal, discard_undo_journal,
  undo_from_snapshot, journal_insert, journal_remove, save_for_undo}` over a one-level
  `UndoJournal` (`crates/fr-board/src/board/snapshot.rs`). **Plan 8 must not replace
  it with a clone.**
- **Plan 7 ruling 5 / scan ruling 8 — one row per ordered container, and the
  comparator's totality is measured, not assumed.** The decision table is
  `crates/fr-router/README.md`'s "Ruling 5's container decisions, confirmed against
  Java". Scan ruling 8's *reason* for making `BatchFanout.Component.Pin` a
  `JavaTreeSet` was half wrong — `:773-775`'s `pinIndex` tie-break runs on **every**
  branch, so an unrecognised sorting order gives pure `pinIndex` order (quirk #220)
  rather than an arbitrary one — and the container is a `JavaTreeSet` anyway, because
  "no board duplicates a pin index" is a property of the *input*: `JavaTreeSet` is
  `java.util.TreeSet` whether or not the input has it, and `BTreeSet` is defined only
  if it does.
- **`scripts/differential/rust` is not rustfmt-clean, and Plan 7 left it that way on
  purpose.** At the start of Plan 7 the crate had 160 `cargo fmt --check` diffs across
  14 pre-existing driver binaries. Plan 7 Task 9 reverted a blanket-fmt commit rather
  than bury it, and **Task 17 decided against a dedicated fmt commit**: the drivers are
  transcriptions of Java bodies, laid out to sit line-for-line beside the Java they
  mirror so a reviewer can diff them by eye, and rustfmt rewraps exactly the long
  argument lists and match arms that alignment depends on. The crate is **not** a
  workspace member (`cargo fmt --all` does not reach it) and nothing ships from it, so
  the cost of the churn — one 160-diff commit through every driver, and every future
  transcription fighting the formatter — buys nothing a reader wants. The workspace
  crates are `cargo fmt --all --check` clean and that is the gate.
- Every deliberate divergence must be greppable: `// not ported:` for skipped
  members, `// totalized:` for crash-to-value changes, `// Java bug:` for
  reproduced defects.
- **Plan 6 controller ruling AE — the one accepted departure from "no static
  mutable state".** `fr_geometry::Line` carries a private `identity: u64` taken
  from a process-wide `AtomicU64`, because Java's `PolylineTrace.change` compares
  two `Line` **objects** with `!=` and the port has to answer the same question
  (quirk #74; the `router-dac2020-bm01` `ripupPassNo >= 2` divergence was nothing
  else). It is accepted, and recorded here and in the plan's amendment block
  rather than passed over, because nothing reads the counter's *value* — only the
  equivalence relation it induces — so no output, ordering, hash or serialised
  form can observe a token and runs stay bit-reproducible; because Java's own
  object identity is process-global mutable state, so this is the closest model
  rather than an invention, and it invents no threading policy; and because every
  alternative (an arena threaded through `Line`'s pure transforms, `Rc`/`Arc`
  pointer identity, a `thread_local!` counter) is materially worse. The token has
  **one** reader, `Line::is_same_object`, which has **one** caller,
  `Board::change_trace`. Anything else using it to stand in for `==` is a bug.
- **Plan 6 controller ruling Y — `JavaTreeSet`, not `BTreeSet`, wherever the
  comparator is not a total order.** Plan 6 ruling 4 said "a sorted set, never a
  `BinaryHeap`", and Task 4 measured what that has to mean: `SortedRoomNeighbour`
  (quirk #160) and `MazeListElement` (quirk #171) both have **non-transitive**
  comparators, and `TreeSet`'s red-black insertion visits a different subset of the
  elements than `BTreeSet`'s B-tree does — so the two disagree about which element
  is dropped as "equal" and in what order the rest come out. Measured on
  `run.sh p6t3` mode 3. `crates/fr-router/src/java_tree_set.rs` is therefore a port
  of `java.util.TreeMap`'s red-black tree (`put`, `deleteEntry`, `fixAfterInsertion`,
  `fixAfterDeletion`), and every `TreeSet` in the router's hot path uses it.
  `BTreeSet` stays correct — and is used — only where the comparator provably is a
  total order.
- **Which `// totalized:` sites earn a row** in the totalization table: the
  **reachable** ones and the **loop-bearing** ones — a site some real input can
  reach, or one where the Java behaviour being replaced is a non-terminating
  loop rather than a throw. Both are facts a reader cannot recover from the
  marker alone. A merely defensive site — an unreachable arm where Java would
  simply throw — stays a comment at the site, unless the *Java* code is
  independently worth reading (the two `DesignRulesChecker` description rows are
  listed on that ground). Under this rule Plan 5's remaining `fr-drc` markers —
  `src/airline.rs:68`, `src/checker.rs:346`, `:362`, `:398`,
  `src/net_incompletes.rs:101`, `:180`, `:269` — get no row: each is an
  unreachable `NullPointerException`/`ClassCastException`/
  `ArrayIndexOutOfBoundsException` arm that `calculateAllIncompletes`' own
  grouping (DesignRulesChecker.java:557-561) rules out. `grep -rn "// totalized:"`
  stays the complete list; the table is the subset a reader must be told about.
- The differential harness lives in `scripts/differential/`. Every driver is a genuine
  Java-vs-Rust pair. `p2t3` is the fixed `ShapeTree`/`MinAreaTree` script that
  `crates/fr-board/tests/min_area_tree.rs` was written from; `p2t3r` is its randomised
  sibling, which also drives `insert_tiles`, `remove_opt` with `null` holes and in-place
  leaf re-keying.

  label #200 — `--max-items` also stops the optimizer) were each re-read against the committed

  discharged in `crates/freerouting/src/cli.rs` and in `crates/freerouting/README.md`, because the
  port has **no** `--max-items` flag of its own: the surface for it is the generic override, which
  is `--set router.max_items=N` on the native form and `--router.max_items=N` on the legacy one

- **Plan 7's quirk table has one row *struck before it was written*.** The plan's
  label #196 said "`getNormalizedScore` divides by `getMaximumScore()`, which is 0
  for a board with no connections, so the board scores NaN". **HEAD does not.**
  `BoardStatistics.java:626-633` opens the method with
  `if (maximumScore <= 0f) { return 0f; }` and a six-line comment saying the guard
  exists so that "comparisons like `score > threshold` work predictably" and to
  prevent "NaN propagation which could silently break stagnation detection". Plan 7
  Task 1 read the file, reproduced the guard, and pinned the inverse assertion in
  `crates/fr-router/tests/score.rs` `an_empty_board_scores_zero_not_nan`
  (`tutorial_board.dsn` is the fixture that reaches it: `maxConnections == 0`).
  There is no NaN to record, so no row was taken for it. Task 1's three rows are
  therefore #194 (the plan's label #195), #195 and #196 — two findings the plan did
  not anticipate — and the plan's label #196 maps to **no id at all**.
- **Plan 6 controller ruling Z — a plan's quirk numbers are *labels*, not ids.**
  Plan 6's task briefs were written with a quirk table numbered #155 upward. By the
  time Task 2 ran, Plan 5's final wave had taken the register to #155, and every
  later brief's numbers were off by a different amount. The ruling: a brief's
  numbers are **plan labels**; the implementer reads the last row of the `pinned`
  table, claims the next free id, and the ledger records the next free id after
  each task. Nothing renumbers a row that has already landed. Task 2's review
  renumbered #160-#162 back to #156-#158 because they had landed the same day; no
  later task did. A plan that wants to pre-assign ids must be written after the
  register it is numbering against is frozen — which no plan can be.

  opens with a bolded "(Plan N obligation)" are not improvement ideas but work
  a later plan *must* do. **As of Plan 5, still open:** the ladder hang's second

- **~~`obligation:`~~ `RoutingJobScheduler.scheduleJob` — the API path composes the merge
  differently. DISCHARGED by Plan 8 Tasks 7 and 12** — the marker had two independent users and
  both are closed. Task 12 (`crates/freerouting/src/mcp/tools/route_board.rs`) composes ruling
  AU's MCP path from `SettingsMerger` in `RoutingJobScheduler.scheduleJob`'s own order, with the
  caller's sparse `settings` object as `ApiSettings` at priority 70, and it is *measured* against
  the CLI: `route_board` answers a session byte-identical to `p8t1`'s
  `tests/reference/cli-tutorial_board/route.ses` and to what `freerouting route` writes for
  `fixtures/Issue143-rpi_splitter.dsn`. Task 7
  (`crates/freerouting/src/commands/drc.rs::quality_score_settings`) is the other user, quirk
  #272's DRC quality score, pinned by `p8t3 merge`. What survives is the *warning*:
  `resolve_headless` models the CLI-started path and only that. Original text follows.
  `fr_settings::resolve_headless` linearises the **CLI-started** two-merge path
  (`Freerouting.java:125-146` then `RoutingJobScheduler.java:103-186`), whose premise is that
  merge #1's result is complete when it is re-injected at priority 70. An API job never runs
  merge #1: `job.routerSettings` is `new RouterSettings()` (`core/RoutingJob.java:105`), the
  request body (`api/v1/JobInputResource.java:203-211`), or a bare `setLayerCount` (`:337-339`,
  `:540-542`), so `ApiSettings` at 70 is a *sparse* override and merge #2's own `0..60` chain does
  the work. Plan 8, which owns the API surface (plan ruling 10), must compose that path from
  `SettingsMerger` directly rather than calling `resolve_headless`. The note is carried in code on
  `crates/fr-settings/src/resolve.rs`'s `resolve_headless`.
- Java's per-flag command-line value normalisation is tabulated separately in
  `docs/cli-legacy-flags.md`; `crates/freerouting/src/legacy.rs` forwards raw
  values and points at that file.
- **`tools/freerouting-2.3.0.jar` and the clone's HEAD differ in more than plan
  ruling 1's fifteen `Keyword` names.** Found in Plan 3 Task 9 while diffing the
  whole 104-file DSN fixture corpus: `DsnFile.readStringScope` gained a resync
  loop between the two. 2.3.0 does `yybegin(NAME); nextString(); nextToken(); if
  (!= CLOSED_BRACKET) warn;` and returns straight away, leaving the scanner
  wherever the stray token left it; HEAD drops the `yybegin` and adds `while
  (nextToken != null && nextToken != CLOSED_BRACKET) nextToken = nextToken();`,
  which resynchronises to the closing bracket. The port follows HEAD, per the
  plan's "Java source authority is the clone's HEAD" constraint. Exactly one
  corpus fixture is affected — `Issue229-display-8-digit-hc595.dsn`, whose
  `(PN "DISPLAY 7-SEG 0.5"")` has an unbalanced string quote. There, 2.3.0
  desynchronises and silently drops everything after the `structure` scope
  (`Success`, 1 item, 0 components, 0 nets), while HEAD — and this port — read
  on and produce 22 components and 30 nets. Verified against both jars with a
  reflective probe. **Task 15's corpus-wide differential runs against 2.3.0 and
  will see this fixture disagree; that disagreement is expected, not a port
  bug.** Minimal repro: a `(place … (PN "A 0.5""))` scope in any otherwise valid
  DSN.
